# Standards, Tools and Templates

*Created: 2025-04-02T21:33:58.280Z by Aaron Kennelly*
*Last Updated: 2025-04-10T17:34:43.835Z by Aaron Kennelly*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/3946840066/Standards+Tools+and+Templates*

---

noneBelow are the guidelines that shape design work, achieve visual consistency, design for accessibility  along with templates and tips for each.
## Design Guidelines and Principles
### Design Principles
By following these principles, we can create designs that are not only beautiful and functional but also deliver a seamless and enjoyable user experience.
#### User-centered Design
Always start with the end-user in mind. Understand their needs, behaviors, and pain points. Use UX Research templates by collaborating with Product Owners and Business Analysts to gather insights that drive decisions. This principle ensures that our designs solve real problems and offer a great experience.
#### Simplicity and Clarity
Strive to keep designs straightforward and easy to understand. Remove any unnecessary elements that could cause confusion. Rely on clear typography, sufficient whitespace, and simple layouts to communicate your message effectively.
#### Consistency
Maintain a uniform look and feel across all areas of the product. Use our Design System Sytle Guild to ensure colors, fonts, and UI elements are the same throughout every design. Consistency builds trust and makes the product easier to use.
#### Accessibility and Inclusivity
Design for everyone, regardless of ability. Use WCAD accessible color contrasts, readable fonts, and clear navigation to make sure all users can interact with our products. Our Accessibility Checklist Template helps verify that designs meet these standards.
#### Scalability
Create designs that can grow with the product. Plan for additional features and future updates by ensuring that your design structure is flexible and adaptable.
#### Collaboration
Foster open communication and teamwork throughout the process. Share you work early and often, and use our Feedback and Iteration Form to gather feedback from teammates and stakeholders. Collaboration leads to better ideas and improved outcomes.
#### Iterative Improvement
Embrace continuous learning and refinement. Test your designs with real users and use their feedback to make small, regular improvements. This iterative process helps ensure that our design evolves and stays effective over time.
### Accessibility and Inclusive Design
Designing for accessibility means ensuring that CFI products are usable by everyone, including people with disabilities. Below outlines our approach to inclusive design.
Adhering to Guidelines
Following established Web Content Accessibility Standards (WCAG) to ensure that our designs are accessible. This includes considerations like color contract, text size, and navigation simplicity.1. Practical Techniques
Color and contrast have been verified for text and important elements have sufficient contrast. Refer to the Templates and Resources section for tools to review designs.

2. Alt text and labels have been provided to development teams for images, icons, labels, and form elements to aid screen reader users.

3. Keyboard navigation has been implemented by development teams to ensure that designed interface elements are fully navigable using a keyboard, ensuring a smooth user experience for users who rely on assistive device

## Design System
A design system is a library of reusable components (buttons, form fields, modals, etc.) and their corresponding design tokens.
The following guidelines have been developed to ensure consistency with components and patterns.
[Design system placeholder] Design Tokens placeholder
## Tools
### Figma (Design & Prototyping)
Figma is a cloud-based design tool that has become a standard for UI/UX design. Its strengths for Agile teams include real-time collaboration and easy sharing via links.
Figma files are organized by project and use pages within files for different flows or iterations. This way, teams can easily find the designs they need.
## Version Control and Design Documentation Standards
Robust version control and clear documentation are essential to maintain consistency, trace changes, and ensure a smooth workflow. This section covers practices and tools we use.
A well-organized Figma file is essential for design excellence. Our Figma files are organized by product responsibility teams to enhance collaboration and project management. Roberto Plascencia will work with me to build out the standards for Figma version control and standards for naming and organization.
Version Control for Design Files
Just like code, design files need version control. We use Figma&rsquo;s versioning features to track changes over time. This practice prevents loss of work and makes it easier to roll back to earlier versions if needed. DO NOT delete screens or flows, archive them for restoration later if necessary.Clear Documentation
Document every design decision with a design rationale document. This should include details on why specific design choices were made, challenges encountered, and how they were addressed. Our Design Decision and Design Review templates provides a structure for capturing these details systematically.
Consistent File Naming and Organization
Standardized naming conventions and file structures make it easy for team members to find and reference files. This reduces confusion and enhances collaboration, especially when multiple people work on the same product.
[Standard for Figma naming conventions].
[Standard Figma file structure].
Change Logs and Approval Processes
We maintain a change log that records all updates to the design files along with dates and reasons for changes. Ensure that significant changes receive proper sign-off using our Handoff Approval Template. This practice fosters accountability and transparency within product teams.
Change log for Specialists

16falsenonelisttrue## UX Designer Cadence Overview
A balanced cadence helps maintain alignment, encourages peer learning, and supports continuous improvement. It comprises daily check-ins, weekly reviews and handoffs, bi-weekly retrospectives, and strategic monthly sessions. Each ceremony is designed to foster collaboration, clarify priorities, and drive design excellence.
### Daily Rituals
#### Daily Stand-up
See Daily Stand-ups below for more information and examples.
| Category | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Cadence | Every weekday (Mon&ndash;Fri) |
| Duration | 15 minutes |
| Purpose | Quick status updates, sharing progress, identifying blockers |
| Preparation | Each participant prepares a &ldquo;Yesterday-Today-Blockers&rdquo; update |
| Participants | All UX Designers; optionally, Product Owners for visibility |
### Weekly Ceremonies
#### Weekly Design Review Session
See Design Reviews below for more information.
| Category | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Cadence | Every Tuesday / Thursday |
| Duration | 60 minutes |
| Purpose | Review design work, gather constructive feedback, align on design quality and consistency. |
| Preparation | 1. Designers share designs/prototypes 24 hours in advance.
2. Review pre-distributed design documentation and prepare discussion points. |
| Participants & Roles | 1. UX Designers: Present their current design iterations and prototypes.
2. Stakeholders (Product Managers, Developer Leads): Provide input from a broader project perspective. |
### Monthly Ceremonies
#### Peer Learning & Knowledge Sharing Session
| Category | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Cadence | Once a month |
| Duration | 60-90 minutes |
| Purpose | Discuss lessons learned, share new design techniques, review case studies, and foster continuous learning. |
| Expected Participants & Roles | Rotating UX Speaker (Peer Leader): Prepares and presents a case study or recent insight. 
All UX Designers: Engage in discussion, ask questions, and share their own experiences. |
| Preparation | Pre-Meeting Preparation (&asymp;30 minutes by the presenter): 
Develop slide deck or visual content using a pre-set template. 
Collect relevant case studies or research articles for discussion. |
#### Retrospective Session
See Sprint Retrospective below for more information.
| Category | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Cadence & Duration | Monthly; 60 minutes |
| Purpose | Reflect on monthly performance, identify wins and opportunities for improvement, discuss process adjustments. |
| Expected Participants | UX Designers, Design Ops Manager, Optional: Product Owners |
| Roles | UX Designers: Provide honest feedback on processes and outcomes. 
Design Ops Manager: Facilitate the discussion and compile insights. 
Optional: Product Owners may join for cross-functional retrospective insights. |
| Preparation | Pre-Meeting Preparation (&asymp;20 minutes): 
- Complete a pre-retrospective survey focusing on &ldquo;What worked well, what didn&rsquo;t, and what to change.&rdquo; 
- Review previous retrospective notes for continuity. |
#### Documentation and Knowledge Management Session
| Category | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Cadence & Duration | Monthly; 45 minutes |
| Purpose | Review, update, and disseminate lessons learned, best practices, and refined playbooks to the entire team. |
| Expected Participants & Roles | UX Designers: Update documentation with personal insights and outcomes.  
Design Lead: Consolidate updates into central knowledge repositories and ensure consistency. |
| Preparation | Pre-Meeting Preparation (&asymp;30 minutes): Gather relevant documentation from the month, compile updates and suggested improvements. |
## Design Reviews
Design reviews are structured processes to gather feedback and evaluate a design against the product requirements and objectives. 
See Design Reviews for specific audiences for the K-12 program.
See Templates for starting a new design review.
| Review Type | What is involved | When it is conducted |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sparring session | Quick feedback on what is working and what needs improvements. | Early in the design phase, when gathering requirements, before the decision are made. |
| Design Review | Review of the usability aspects, ensuring it will work as intended to meet user needs and business goals | Before and during development. |
| Usability Review | Heuristic evaluation to make sure the design is easy to use without the ability to test with users to catch usability issues. | Before and during development. Once a set of screens / flow / prototype is ready, before the final design gets approved. |
## Agile Scrum and Product Design
### Why integrate UX into Scrum? 
Agile methodologies empower our team to work flexibly and iteratively. Embedding design activities into Scrum events reduces the risk of late design and development changes, rework, and &ldquo;Debt&rdquo; (design and development deficiencies that need fixing later). When design teams are involved throughout, teams maintain a holistic view of user needs, make better product decisions, and avoid the development of features that are difficult to use. The outcomes are higher quality products that meet both business objectives and user expectations, delivered on time.
### Overview
This section details each Scrum ceremony and activity, illustrating where and how design fits in. Included is best practices for incorporating design into backlog refinement, sprint planning, daily stand-ups, development work, sprint reviews, and retrospectives. We also discuss handling design-specific challenges like managing design debt, coordinating dual-track discovery and delivery, and integrating research insights into product decisions. 
### Dual-track Product Design
Modern agile teams increasingly adopt a dual-track approach. One track for continuous discovery (ux research and design) and one for delivery (implementation), both feeding into a single product backlog.
Dual-track agile model illustrating continuous Product Discovery (ideation, prototyping, user testing) in parallel with Product Execution (development sprints). This approach was popularized by Marty Cagen and Jeff Patton and ensures that discovery is a continuous part of development.  Product management focuses on value, design on usability, and engineering on feasibility at each stage.
Product discovery is not a one-time phase of research, but an ongoing activity parallel to Scrum sprints. Treating discovery as an upfront project can lead to outdated assumptions and a loss of agility. Additionally, trying to do all discovery within each development sprint without any overlap can overstretch designers &ldquo;feeding the beast&rdquo; by always scrambling one sprint ahead.
The recommended approach is to maintain a continuous discovery cadence that informs and improves delivery, without being completely decoupled. In practice, this means the Scrum team is always doing some design work for the current sprint&rsquo;s stories (e.g. supporting implementation, refining designs) and some design work for upcoming sprints (e.g. research, prototyping new ideas). By overlapping these activities, the team can validate ideas early (kill or pivot them if needed) and ensure that when development picks up a user story, the design is user-validated and ready. 
This discovery track is collaborative with product owners, business analysts, and members of the development team. This transparency and involvement in discovery activities whenever possible. For example, observing user interviews or participation in design ideation sessions, so insights permeate the whole team.
### Backlog and User-centric Stories
All work lives in the product backlog. The backlog should be a user-centric artifact composed of user stories, improvement ideas, and research &ldquo;spikes&rdquo;, prioritized by user and business value. Each story should encapsulate a goal or need from an end-user perspective, defined collaboratively by the product owner, business analysts, and design roles. These techniques like user-story mapping (described below) are extremely useful at the onset to visualize the user&rsquo;s journey and ensure the backlog covers all necessary interactions. Story maps spark team collaboration and provide the &ldquo;bigger picture&rdquo; of how the product fits together, preventing the team from losing sight of context when working on discrete backlog items.
### Design as part of Definition of Done
To successfully integrate design, teams often update their working agreements such as &ldquo;Definition of Ready&rdquo; and &ldquo;Definition of Done&rdquo;. For example, a story might not be ready to enter a sprint until Design acceptance criteria and assets are attached. It might not be &ldquo;done&rdquo; until the implemented feature passes a usability review by the designer. Ensure user-facing backlog items meet key design criteria before development starts requires:
- clear user story and acceptance criteria

- available visuals / design specifications

- user testing of the solution with no major issues

- adherence to the design system specifications

- and review by the UX, development leader, and product owner

### Approach
Scrum + UX V1 Scrum.org by Jeff Gothelf, a framework that spells out &mdash; step by step &mdash; how Scrum and UX design integrate.
### Product Backlog and User Story Mapping
Every great agile product begins with a well-groomed product backlog. Designers play a key role from the start by contributing user insights, personas, and workflows that shape backlog items. This often starts with a discovery phase, a time before development sprints begin, where the team explores user needs. During this phase, designers conduct stakeholder interviews, field studies, or analyze existing software and workflows to uncover pain points and inefficiencies. The insights are used to create artifacts like user archetypes (a representation of key user groups), journey maps (a visualization of end-to-end user tasks), and initial wireframes or concepts for the solution. These help the team and client establish a shared understanding of user goals in conjunction with business goals.
One highly recommended activity at this stage is user-story mapping. Originally popularized by Jeff Patton&rsquo;s book, user story mapping is a collaborative exercise where the team maps out user activities and tasks in a visual layout (often using sticky notes, either physical or digital)​. The top row of a story map lists the high-level user activities (the big steps a user takes to achieve their goals), and beneath each are the detailed user stories or sub-tasks needed to support that activity​.

Story mapping helps ensure the backlog has complete coverage of the user&rsquo;s workflow, identifies gaps, and prioritizes what&rsquo;s most critical. It also replaces traditional lengthy requirements documents with an interactive, team-owned map that is easier to update as things change. The outcome of a story mapping session is a structured backlog where each item is tied to user value and context.
#### Best practices for backlog creation and refinement with Design
##### Collaborate on Writing User Stories
Product owners or business analysts typically draft user stories, but design practitioners should collaborate in this process. Ensure each story clearly states the user benefit (e.g., &ldquo;As a Specialist, I want to search for a client by name so that I can quickly locate their records&rdquo;). UX can help rephrase technical requests into user-centric stories and check that acceptance criteria include UX considerations (such as usability requirements or accessibility needs). This collaboration prevents user stories from being too system-focused and keeps them tied to real user scenarios.
##### Incorporate Research and Data
Backlog items should be informed by evidence. Designers and researchers should share insights like user pain points frequency, support ticket data, or findings from usability tests on an earlier system. For example, if research shows that &ldquo;80% of Specialist find the current reporting dashboard confusing,&rdquo; that should drive priority for redesigning that module and be referenced in the user story for it. Backlog refinement meetings are a good forum to inject these insights so that the team and stakeholders understand why a story is important. Using data to drive prioritization fosters an outcome-focused mindset rather than an output of features.
##### User Story Mapping for Epic Planning
Whenever the team starts a new epic or large feature set, consider running a quick user story mapping workshop. This could involve the whole Scrum team plus subject matter experts from the client. Designer or Product Owners can facilitate the session by sketching the user&rsquo;s journey and ensuring the discussion stays focused on user goals. The result can be documented as a diagram or kept as a living digital board on Confluence or Miro.
##### Backlog Refinement
Backlog refinement a.k.a. &ldquo;Grooming&rdquo; is an ongoing activity, typically a mid-sprint meeting to review upcoming stories. Always include UX roles in backlog refinement sessions. In these meetings, the product owner re-prioritizes and the team discusses each candidate story for the next sprint​. Designers can contribute by quickly sketching solutions on a whiteboard or Miro board during the discussion to help the team visualize the story. If a story is unclear or too large, the team might decide to split it, a designer could propose how to slice it from a user-flow perspective (perhaps delivering a basic version of a feature first and enhancing later). Design participation often leads to the identification of &ldquo;Spikes&rdquo;, an exploratory tasks for the team to get answers to open questions​. 
For example, a spike might be &ldquo;Research how users navigate the dashboard&rdquo; or &ldquo;Prototype the new form design and test with 3 users,&rdquo; which would then be added to the backlog. By grooming the backlog together, the team ensures upcoming work has enough detail and the design aspect is well-understood and ready.
##### Definition of Ready
As mentioned earlier, teams should agree on what conditions a user story must meet to be ready for a sprint. From a design standpoint, before a story is taken into a sprint, it should ideally have a lo-fi or hi-fi design attached and directly linked in a Figma file. Additional attachments might include any necessary user research (like a quick user validation of the design concept) completed or at least planned, and clear acceptance criteria including design requirements.
For example, an acceptance criterion might state &ldquo;Form fields are aligned and follow error-handling guidelines&rdquo; or &ldquo;User can successfully complete the task in under 2 minutes (based on usability test)&rdquo;. These set expectations for both designers and developers. This might sound like a lot but it dramatically reduces confusion during the sprint. It&rsquo;s fine if minor design tweaks occur later but the core user flow should not be a surprise during development. Designers should work closely with product owners to enforce a &ldquo;ready&rdquo; state protects the team from pulling in work that isn&rsquo;t fully thought out​.
### Sprint Planning
Sprint planning is the ceremony that kicks off each sprint. Sprints run two weeks in duration for our project teams. During sprint planning, the product owner will present the top priority stories (already refined) and the team will discuss and estimate them.
For each user story, identify what UX work is needed either within that story or alongside it. For example, a story like &ldquo;Add ability for supervisors to approve timesheets&rdquo; might involve UX sub-tasks such as &ldquo;Design the approval screen states&rdquo; or &ldquo;Usability test the approval flow with 2 users&rdquo;. The team needs to account for this effort. 
K-12 Product Teams create separate tickets for UX work that correspond to the development stories. For example, an Epic might have a story &ldquo;Design and user-test the provider approval feature&rdquo; assigned to a designer, and another story &ldquo;Implement provider approval feature&rdquo; for developers, both linked to the same Epic. It is a best practice to schedule these design stories ahead of the development story so that design is completed ahead implementation.

The key is visibility, everyone on the team should see the design tasks on the sprint board so they are recognized as part of the sprint scope​. This prevents a scenario where developers start a story but are waiting on design or where design changes aren&rsquo;t communicated until late. In sprint planning, after the team agrees to take on a story, designers should speak up to outline what design/research work is involved and ensure it&rsquo;s added to the plan.#### Estimating Effort
Scrum teams use story points for estimating user stories. It&rsquo;s important to clarify how much design work factors into estimates. A recommended technique for designers is to use T-shirt sizing (S, M, L, XL) for tasks. During planning the designer might say &ldquo;This story&rsquo;s design effort is Medium&rdquo; which implies a certain expected workload, without conflating it with the developers&rsquo; point estimates. 
A &ldquo;S&rdquo; (small) might mean only minor UI tweaks or no new design needed whereas an &ldquo;L&rdquo; (large) means a significant design undertaking, possibly so large that the designer can&rsquo;t commit to any additional work that sprint​. T-shirt sizes are a lightweight way to ensure the designer or researcher doesn&rsquo;t get overcommitted relative to the developers.
NNGroup using T-shirt sizes to estimate design work. This method keeps design effort visible without skewing the development team&rsquo;s velocity.
#### Align on Sprint Goals that Include Design Outcomes
When formulating the sprint goal, include the user value and any user experience outcome expected. For instance, instead of a technical goal like &ldquo;Implement ticketing module&rdquo;, frame it as &ldquo;Enable Specialists to submit and approve task items (validated with user feedback).&rdquo; This phrasing reminds the team that a successful sprint isn&rsquo;t just coding the feature, it&rsquo;s making sure users can actually use it effectively. 
If the sprint includes a research spike then write, &ldquo;Validate new navigation menu with users&rdquo; so that learning can be part of the goal as well. This keeps discovery work tied to sprint objectives and not seen as a separate &ldquo;nice-to-have.&rdquo;
#### Coordinate for Design to Work Ahead of Development
Part of the designer&rsquo;s work should be at least one sprint ahead of development of major features.  During sprint planning for development, also acknowledge what design will be doing for the next sprint&rsquo;s stories. 
For example, &ldquo;This sprint the dev team builds feature X, and meanwhile UX Designer will be finalizing designs for feature Y coming next.&rdquo; This is essential when using a dual-track approach within your sprint cadence. This ensures everyone knows, for instance, that the prototype for the next feature must be ready by the end of the current sprint.
When the work is complex, hold a mini &ldquo;UX planning&rdquo; session in addition to the main sprint planning.  The important part is to synchronize and provide clear communication that when a designer working ahead, they should periodically check back with the developers to verify technical feasibility and with the PO to ensure the concept still aligns with priorities.

By the end of sprint planning, you should have a clear scope that includes both features to build and Design activities to execute. Everyone on the team knows what the designer(s) and researcher(s) will be doing, and how that ties into the developers&rsquo; work. This level of upfront alignment sets the sprint on a firm footing.### Daily Stand-ups and Ongoing Collaboration During the Sprint
Once a sprint is underway, ongoing coordination is critical. Scrum&rsquo;s Stand-up ceremonies are usually a 15-minute team sync to ensure progress and surface blockers. To maintain alignment with the product team, design members should attend all stand-ups even if they are working ahead on future items. When Design skips stand-ups, the team risks miscommunication and might implement things that don&rsquo;t match the design intent, leading to waste and Design debt.
During stand-ups, each team member typically answers the three questions: &ldquo;What did I do yesterday? What will I do today? What (if anything) is blocking me?&rdquo; An example of design answers are:
Yesterday
&ldquo;Yesterday I created wireframes for the new dashboard and reviewed them with product owners for quick feedback,&rdquo; or &ldquo;I collaborated with the BA to refine the error messages for the form validation.&rdquo; This informs the developers what&rsquo;s coming or what changed. Design can also mention any cross-functional collaboration such as &ldquo;I worked with (developer) on tweaking the CSS for the modal dialog&rdquo;. 
This shows the team that design-dev collaboration is happening. If any user insight was obtained (say from a user test or a stakeholder demo), briefly share it. &ldquo;I discovered that Specialists are confused by the term &lsquo;Account ID&rsquo;, we might need to rename it, I&rsquo;ll update the story.&rdquo; These little knowledge drops ensure the whole team stays user-focused daily.
Today
&ldquo;Today I will finalize the high-fidelity mockups for the profile page and post them on Figma for review,&rdquo; or &ldquo;This afternoon I&rsquo;m conducting a 1-hour usability test with two specialist on the messaging app. I invite anyone interested to observe.&rdquo; If the designer needs input or plans to do a workshop or whiteboard session, mention it. &ldquo;I&rsquo;d like to grab 30 minutes with the front-end developer to discuss error states &ndash; I&rsquo;ll set up a quick Miro board after stand-up.&rdquo; By announcing these, you not only hold yourself accountable but also invite collaboration.
Blockers
Call out anything impeding design progress​. Common blockers might be waiting on a decision &ldquo;I need the product owner to clarify the requirement for printing reports&rdquo;, technical constraints &ldquo;Figma export is giving me issues, might need dev help to use a different approach for prototyping&rdquo;, or scheduling issues &ldquo;I haven&rsquo;t been able to recruit a user for testing this week&rdquo;. 
The Scrum Master should treat UX blockers with the same urgency as development blockers. For example, if the designer can&rsquo;t get a test user, maybe the Business Analyst or Product Owner can help reach out to a customer network. Another scenario might be design is waiting on some usage data to make a decision, perhaps a developer can pull log data to help. By surfacing these in stand-up, the team can swarm to resolve them, keeping the sprint on track.
Actively listening is just as critical. Design should also listen to what developers report. Sometimes a developer&rsquo;s blocker might be &ldquo;I&rsquo;m not sure how this should look or behave&rdquo;, that&rsquo;s a cue for the designer to step in after the stand-up to clarify the design. Perhaps the dev didn&rsquo;t realize a spec was attached to the story, or maybe a state wasn&rsquo;t covered. 
By attending stand-ups, Design can quickly catch these issues and offer help &ldquo;I&rsquo;ll schedule a quick review with you right after this to go over that form behavior&rdquo;. This prevents mis-implementation. It also helps Designers hear about any technical challenges that might require a design adjustment. For example if a developer says &ldquo;The chart library can&rsquo;t do that interaction&rdquo; then the designer should propose an alternative.
### Design-Development Collaboration
The daily stand-up is the heartbeat of team communication but collaboration goes beyond just those meetings. Let&rsquo;s outline ongoing Design-Development collaboration practices throughout the sprint.
#### Open Communication Channels
A best practice is to have a dedicated Teams channel where questions and quick discussions can happen throughout the day. Designers should share early work in this channel for feedback such as posting a screenshot or a Figma link with a message &ldquo;Here&rsquo;s a first draft of the new form layout, any thoughts?&rdquo;. Developers can ping the designer if something in the implementation doesn&rsquo;t match the mockup or if they have an idea to improve it. Keeping these conversations visible to the whole team (opposed to siloed private chats) means others can chime in. Sometimes a QA or PO will notice something and add valuable input. It also reinforces a culture of transparency.
#### Design-Development Handoff or Hand-in-Hand
In traditional Waterfall methodologies, teams talked about &ldquo;handoff&rdquo; &ndash; the moment design deliverables are given to dev. In Scrum with integrated UX, it&rsquo;s less a one-time handoff and more a continuous collaboration. Regardless, certain touch points are critical. 
For example, once a Figma design is ready for a story, the designer should gather the developers via a short call to walk through the design. This is essentially a mini design review session. During this time, the designer can explain user context &ldquo;This is for the Specialist persona who often multitasks&hellip;&rdquo;, point out interactions &ldquo;When the user clicks edit, this section expands&hellip;&rdquo;, and highlight things like responsive behavior or error scenarios. Devs can ask questions and confirm feasibility. 
This exchange, which might only take 15 minutes, can save hours of guesswork later. The design system and associated Figma files use components and style libraries so developers have a consistent reference for spacings, colors, etc.
### Mid-Sprint Check-ins and Reviews
 It&rsquo;s helpful to schedule at least one mid-sprint checkpoint dedicated to Design, often informally. For example around mid-sprint, the team can do a quick design QA on what&rsquo;s been built so far.  The UX designer reviews the working software to see if it matches the design and provides feedback early, rather than waiting for the end of sprint. This can be as simple as the developer screen-sharing with the designer and going through the UI together. Any discrepancies can be logged as bugs or quick fixes. 
This is also a chance to evaluate if the design itself needs tweaking now that it&rsquo;s in the real product. Perhaps something looked good in Figma but feels clunky in the browser. If so, the designer can adjust the design on the spot with the developer&rsquo;s input. This tight feedback loop is the essence of Agile Design, iterating quickly based on real implementation feedback.
#### User Feedback
Whenever possible, try to get actual user feedback on what you&rsquo;re building before you begin building. Direct end-users might be difficult to reach frequently. When this occurs, leverage proxy users and / or work with stakeholders to leverage their next and help recruit users. 
For example, if a small feature is done by day seven of a ten-day sprint, the Designer or Product Owner might demo a prototype to a friendly user or stakeholder on day eight and gather impressions. This doesn&rsquo;t replace a formal usability testing or sprint review but it can catch glaring usability issues early. 
If the feedback indicates a simple change &ldquo;Users didn&rsquo;t notice the save button, we should make it more prominent&rdquo;, the team can possibly incorporate that within the sprint. If it&rsquo;s a bigger insight, at least it&rsquo;s captured for next sprint&rsquo;s backlog. Teams practicing continuous delivery sometimes even release features to a subset of users mid-sprint and use analytics or feedback to guide finishing touches. The guiding principle us don&rsquo;t wait if you have an opportunity to validate sooner.
#### Keep Discovery as an Ongoing Activity
While the sprint team is executing on the committed stories, UX Designers might be concurrently running discovery for future work in conjunction with Business Analysts. It&rsquo;s important the rest of the team is aware of this ongoing discovery. One practice is to have a brief slot in a couple of stand-ups each week where the Designer shares updates from any tasks in progress.
An example might be &ldquo;I interviewed 3 users about the new reporting module, here&rsquo;s a 1-minute summary of what I&rsquo;m hearing...&rdquo;. This keeps everyone connected to real user voices and primes them for upcoming features. It also prevents the &ldquo;big reveal&rdquo; anti-pattern where a Designer works for weeks and then dumps a huge report that nobody has context for. Instead, it trickles insights continuously. 
Treat findings as deliverables in the sprint. A story like &ldquo;User test prototype of feature Y&rdquo; is done when the findings are documented and perhaps recommendation are made. The output might be a Confluence page or PowerPoint of results. This way, discovery items have tangible outcomes just like features do.
Overall, the sprint execution phase should feel like a continuous conversation between Design , Product Owners, and Development. When done right, developers start seeing UX Design colleagues as indispensable partners who help ensure what they build will delight users and reduce rework. Product designers/researchers see developers as creative problem-solvers who can inform design possibilities. There&rsquo;s mutual respect and a shared goal for delivering a usable increment by sprint&rsquo;s end.
### Sprint Review
The Sprint Review, also called a Sprint Demo, is typically held on the last day of the sprint. This meeting might include not just the Scrum team but also stakeholders like product sponsors, client representatives, or end-user champions. The purpose is to inspect the increment (the working software completed in the sprint) and adapt the backlog based on feedback. 
For Design, the sprint review is an opportunity to validate the user experience with stakeholders and capture any new insights. It&rsquo;s also a chance to showcase the team&rsquo;s user-centered approach, reinforcing confidence that the product is being built with end-users in mind. Below are some best practices for Design during Sprint Reviews:
##### Prepare Scenarios to Demo
Rather than just listing features, frame the review as a series of user scenarios or tasks being demonstrated. For example, if the sprint delivered a &ldquo;task approval&rdquo; feature, the Product Owner or a team member can present &ldquo;Let&rsquo;s walk through how a supervisor approves a timesheet. As you can see, they go to the pending list, open a task, review it, and click Approve which now triggers a confirmation message.&rdquo; 
This narrative approach, often supported by the Designer&rsquo;s guidance, ensures the demo stays focused on user value and technical details. The Design team can help script these scenarios to highlight improved workflows or design elements that were added for usability. If the designer or researcher conducted any user tests during the sprint, those results could be briefly mentioned. For example, &ldquo;We tested this flow with two supervisors and they completed it without confusion, which is a big improvement from the old system.&rdquo; Such commentary ties the demo to real user validation.
##### Include UX Artifacts
A best practice when applicable is to show a before-and-after comparison or a design artifact to put the sprint work in context. For example, if the team redesigned a screen based on user feedback, you might show a slide with the old vs new design and explain the changes. A powerpoint presentation is a common communication vehicle for stakeholders. Be mindful to not overdo slides, the emphasis should remain on working software but a couple of visuals can clarify the story.
If not all parts of a feature could be built, the Designer might show a Figma prototype of the remaining pieces to stakeholders to set expectations for what&rsquo;s coming. Clients and non-technical stakeholders often appreciate seeing wireframes or journey maps that explain the why behind a feature. For example, showing a mini archetype &ldquo;Meet Alice, a School clerk who will use this feature&hellip;&rdquo; can humanize the demo. These touches, prepared by the Design team make the review more engaging and aligned to user needs.
##### Encourage Stakeholder Interaction
When possible, let a stakeholder or proxy user use the demo. For example, have a client representative actually click through the new interface while the team observes. This is essentially a form of informal usability testing in a live setting. It can reveal unexpected user responses, maybe the stakeholder hesitates because a button label is unclear. These observations are invaluable feedback. 
Make sure the UX Design or BA is noting any issues or suggestions that come up. The Scrum Master or Product Owner should facilitate feedback, asking questions like &ldquo;How does this new process feel to you compared to the old one?&rdquo; or &ldquo;Is there anything you expected to see that&rsquo;s missing?&rdquo; Take note of comments, even if they are change requests. These insights can be turned into new backlog items after the meeting. This aligns with the Agile principle of welcoming changing requirements based on feedback.
##### Show Metrics and User Experience Outcomes
If the team has been tracking experience related metrics, report on them in the review. For example, &ldquo;Our goal was to reduce the steps for task X from 5 to 3. We achieved that and in preliminary tests users completed it 30% faster​.&rdquo; Another examples could be &ldquo;We addressed 4 design debt items this sprint, such as increasing font sizes for readability as required by accessibility guidelines.&rdquo;
##### Capture New User Stories
A sprint review often surfaces new ideas. Perhaps a stakeholder says, &ldquo;This is great, but can we also have a filter on this screen?&rdquo; The Product Owner should capture that as a new user story or note to refine later. Design can help by quickly assessing the idea. Perhaps the designer responds &ldquo;A filter is feasible, we considered it and can revisit that design.&rdquo; Another example might be &ldquo;Our discovery indicated filters were desired, so it&rsquo;s on our roadmap.&rdquo; 
This shows stakeholders their feedback is heard and already in the loop. Be mindful not to promise unplanned features on the spot. Acknowledge the suggestions and put it in the backlog for prioritization. If certain feedback is critical (e.g., a stakeholder finds a particular flow confusing), the Product Owner might even adjust priority of an upcoming story. 
After the sprint review, the Scrum team should have a refreshed understanding of stakeholder expectations and user needs. The product backlog is likely updated with new stories or reprioritized items based on what was learned. The team celebrates what was accomplished and then moves to the final ceremony of the sprint: the Sprint Retrospective. That&rsquo;s where we examine our process, including how UX integration went, and find ways to improve.
### Sprint Retrospective
The Sprint Retrospective is a critical meeting for continuous improvement, usually held after the review/demo and before the next sprint starts. In this meeting, the team reflects on the sprint that just happened and identifies what went well, what didn&rsquo;t, and what can be improved. It&rsquo;s important that Design considerations are part of this reflection. Integrating UX is an ongoing journey, and retrospectives help fine-tune that integration over time.
### Managing UX Debt and Continuous Discovery



# Organizing Figma

*Created: 2025-05-05T18:39:47.722Z by Roberto Plascencia*
*Last Updated: 2025-05-20T19:45:52.485Z by Roberto Plascencia*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/3999727651/Organizing+Figma*

---

To create a consistent introduction for the Figma design files across all personas and designs, consider the following structure:
Introduction to Figma Design Files
Welcome to our Figma design files repository. This collection is organized to ensure a seamless experience for all users, regardless of their persona. Each design file is structured to maintain consistency in layout, style, and functionality. Below is an overview of the organization and layout for each design file:
1. File Naming Convention: Each file is named according to its purpose and target persona, making it easy to identify the relevant designs at a glance.

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
|  |  |
1. Artboard Structure: Each design file contains multiple artboards, each dedicated to a specific screen or component. Artboards are labeled clearly to indicate their function, such as "Home," "Profile," "Settings," etc.
Every design file must include a Cover Page that prominently features the persona and the title of the page.

The designs should be positioned beneath the cover page, focusing solely on the content outlined in the page title. Multiple designs may be included on the page, provided they effectively illustrate a flow or prototype.
1. Component Library: A shared component library is included in each file, containing reusable elements such as buttons, icons, and form fields. This ensures uniformity across different designs and personas.

2. Style Guide: Each file incorporates a style guide that outlines typography, color schemes, and spacing guidelines. This helps maintain visual consistency and aligns with our branding standards.

3. User Flows: User flows are documented within the files to illustrate the intended navigation and interactions for each persona. This aids in understanding the user journey and enhances usability.

4. Annotations and Comments: Designers are encouraged to add annotations and comments directly within the Figma files to provide context and rationale for design decisions, facilitating collaboration and feedback.

By adhering to this organizational structure, we aim to create a cohesive design experience that meets the needs of all users while promoting efficiency and clarity in our design process.
Installing the Design System Assets in Figma
1. In Figma, click on Assets located on the left-hand side, then select Browse team libraries.

2. A modal will appear. Type "test design system" (note that the name will change soon) in the search bar at the top left, and then select Add to file.

1. Once added, the left-hand side of Figma will display the cover page of the assets labeled as &ldquo;most recent design system.&rdquo; Click on that title card to freely browse through the latest assets.



# Checklist for UX Team: Design System Consistency Check

*Created: 2025-08-27T15:37:17.578Z by Roberto Plascencia*
*Last Updated: 2025-08-27T15:40:21.402Z by Roberto Plascencia*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4228382722/Checklist+for+UX+Team+Design+System+Consistency+Check*

---

### Checklist for UX Team: Design System Consistency Check
Objective: To ensure all components and patterns within the design system are consistent, cohesive, and aligned with the brand's principles and user needs.
#### I. Foundational Elements
- Color Palette:
[ ] All colors used are defined in the design system.

- [ ] Color names are semantic (e.g., $color-primary-500, $color-feedback-danger).

- [ ] Accessibility checks (WCAG 2.1) for contrast ratios are performed on all color combinations.

- [ ] Usage guidelines for each color (e.g., for background, text, accent) are clearly documented.

Typography:
- [ ] All fonts used are from the approved typography scale.

- [ ] Heading styles (H1, H2, H3, etc.) and body text styles are consistently applied.

- [ ] Line height, letter spacing, and font weights are standardized for each style.

- [ ] Usage guidelines (e.g., when to use which heading level, max character count for body text) are documented.

Spacing & Layout:
- [ ] A consistent 8-point (or 4-point, etc.) grid system is used across all designs.

- [ ] Spacing tokens (e.g., $spacing-xs, $spacing-lg) are consistently applied for margins, padding, and gaps.

- [ ] All layouts adhere to defined container widths and breakpoints for responsiveness.

Iconography:
- [ ] All icons used are from the approved icon library.

- [ ] Icons share a consistent visual style (e.g., stroke weight, fill style, corner radius).

- [ ] Naming conventions for icons are standardized (e.g., icon-name-16px).

- [ ] Accessibility attributes (e.g., alt text, ARIA labels) are documented for each icon.

#### II. Component Consistency
- Naming Conventions:
[ ] Component names are clear, concise, and follow a standardized naming convention (e.g., Button, Card-Product, Modal-Dialog).

- [ ] All variations (states, sizes, etc.) of a component are documented under the primary component name.

State Management:
- [ ] All components have defined states: Default, Hover, Active, Focus, Disabled, Loading.

- [ ] The visual representation of each state is consistent across all similar components (e.g., the hover state for all buttons looks and feels similar).

Functionality & Behavior:
- [ ] The expected user interaction (e.g., a button should be clickable and have a clear action) is consistent across similar components.

- [ ] All components that trigger an action (e.g., a form submission) have a consistent feedback mechanism (e.g., success message, error state).

Sizing & Responsiveness:
- [ ] Component sizes (e.g., small, medium, large) are standardized and documented.

- [ ] All components are tested and documented for how they behave across different screen sizes (desktop, tablet, mobile).

#### III. Documentation & Usage
- Guidelines:
[ ] Each component has clear "Dos and Don'ts" for its usage.

- [ ] Accessibility notes are included for each component.

- [ ] Code snippets (e.g., for HTML/CSS, React, Vue) are provided and are up-to-date.

Visual Examples:
- [ ] All component variations are visually represented in the documentation.

- [ ] Live demos or interactive examples are available for complex components.

Review & Governance:
- [ ] A clear process is in place for proposing, reviewing, and approving new components or updates to the design system.

- [ ] The design system is regularly audited to ensure consistency and identify any outdated components.

#### IV. Collaboration & Adoption
- Communication:
[ ] Updates to the design system are clearly communicated to the entire team (designers, developers, product managers).

- [ ] A central channel (e.g., Slack, Teams) is designated for questions and feedback on the design system.

Tooling:
- [ ] All designers are using the same source of truth for the design system (e.g., a shared Figma library, Sketch Library).

- [ ] Developers are using the correct component library/package in their projects.

Team Knowledge:
- [ ] All team members are familiar with the design system's principles and guidelines.

- [ ] Training sessions or workshops on how to use the design system are held periodically.

How to use this checklist:
1. Scope the Audit: Decide if you are checking the entire design system or a specific section (e.g., all form elements).

2. Assign Roles: Assign different sections of the checklist to team members.

3. Review: Go through each item one by one and mark it as complete. If an item is not met, note the inconsistencies found.

4. Document Findings: Create a shared document (e.g., a spreadsheet) to track all inconsistencies and areas for improvement.

5. Prioritize & Action: Discuss the findings and prioritize the most critical fixes. Create a plan and timeline to address the identified issues.

# Application UI and Component Strategy

*Created: 2025-11-14T15:15:55.808Z by Matt Vaughn*
*Last Updated: 2025-11-14T15:28:31.341Z by Matt Vaughn*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4461035529/Application+UI+and+Component+Strategy*

---

# IT Summary
> Subject: Status Update on Angular UI Modernization and Component Strategy
Our Angular application modernization project currently operates using a hybrid UI strategy following the transition from PrimeNG to Material Design.
Core Status:
1. Primary Components: The application successfully utilizes Material Design for most general UI controls and aesthetic theming, aligning with the target modernization goal.

2. Layout System: The underlying responsive layout remains dependent on the PrimeNG Flex Grid system. A decision has been finalized to retain the Flex Grid system in its current state, deferring any refactoring efforts (e.g., switching to Bootstrap) to avoid disruption and cost at this time.

3. Component Strategy (Hybrid Model): We have adopted a flexible component strategy to address high-complexity functional needs efficiently. Going forward, PrimeNG UI controls will be used circumstantially to fulfill advanced UI behaviors&mdash;such as the required complex table control with bulk edit features for the new School Roster management system&mdash;where equivalent Material Design components are unavailable or would require excessive custom development.

In essence, the project is maintaining a stable foundation built on the original PrimeNG Flex Grid while using Material Design for aesthetics and leveraging PrimeNG&rsquo;s specialized component library selectively for critical, complex data features.



# Identity Provider (CIAM) Options Analysis

*Created: 2025-05-21T18:16:08.228Z by Robert Beasy*
*Last Updated: 2025-05-21T18:23:32.229Z by Robert Beasy*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4032725075/Identity+Provider+CIAM+Options+Analysis*

---

Key Points
- It seems likely that Microsoft Entra ID (Azure AD B2C) is the best CIAM option for SEAA's scholarship application, given its cost-effectiveness and integration with Azure.

- Research suggests Okta/Auth0 is also a strong contender, especially for its user-friendly admin interface and pre-built identity proofing integrations, but it may be more expensive.

- The legacy custom auth solution is likely not suitable due to security and compliance risks, particularly with FedRAMP/NIST requirements.

- An unexpected detail is that Microsoft Entra ID B2C offers the first 50,000 monthly active users (MAU) free, potentially saving significant costs during off-peak periods.

Why Microsoft Entra ID Stands Out
SEAA's scholarship management application, hosted in Azure, needs to handle up to 100,000 external users with bursty usage patterns, ensure maximum security, and be easily maintained by less technical staff. Microsoft Entra ID B2C seems to fit well, offering FedRAMP compliance and seamless Azure integration, which simplifies management. Its pricing model, with the first 50,000 MAU free and additional MAU at a low rate, aligns with the application's seasonal usage, potentially reducing costs significantly during low-activity months.
Cost and Scalability Considerations
Given the bursty usage (high in August-September, low otherwise), Microsoft Entra ID B2C's pay-as-you-go model is cost-effective. For example, during peak months with 100,000 MAU, costs are minimal compared to Okta, which could charge significantly more for the same user base. This makes it a practical choice for budget optimization, especially for a government application with fluid external users.
Admin Experience and Features
While Okta offers a more user-friendly admin console, Microsoft Entra ID can be managed through the Azure portal with training, which is feasible given SEAA's existing Azure familiarity. Both support self-service features like password resets and account creation, and role management, but Microsoft Entra ID's integration with Azure functions enhances backend connectivity, which is crucial for the application.
Survey Note: Comprehensive Analysis of CIAM Options for SEAA's Scholarship Application
This analysis evaluates Customer Identity and Access Management (CIAM) options for SEAA's government application, which assigns scholarship money to low-income households and manages schools and vendors receiving these funds. The application, hosted in Azure, must be operational within six months, support dozens of state employees and up to 100,000 external users, ensure maximum security with FedRAMP/NIST compliance, and be easily maintained by less technical staff. The primary options are Okta/Auth0, Microsoft Entra ID (Azure AD B2C), and a legacy custom auth solution, with consideration for other alternatives like ForgeRock/Ping Identity and AWS Cognito.
Background and Requirements
SEAA, already using Okta for internal authentication, needs a separate CIAM platform for security separation and specialized capabilities. Key requirements include:
- Cloud deployment in Azure with FedRAMP and NIST 800-53/63 compliance.

- Scalability for 100,000 external users, with bursty usage (high in August-September, low otherwise).

- Self-service features for account creation, password resets, and role management.

- Easy administration for non-technical staff, with clear documentation and videos.

- Integration with external services and support for auditing, analytics, and user behavior tracking.

- Cost optimization, given external users average 4 years of activity and log in fewer than 100 times annually.

Option Evaluation
1. Okta/Auth0 (Customer Identity Cloud)

Okta, a leading identity provider, offers Customer Identity Cloud (formerly Auth0) for consumer-facing applications, with FedRAMP Moderate and High authorizations. It supports OIDC/OAuth2, SAML, social logins (Google, Apple), and adaptive MFA, with a rich integration network for identity proofing (e.g., LexisNexis, http://ID.me ). The admin UI is user-friendly, supporting self-service features and role management via groups, with APIs for creating roles dynamically. Scalability is proven for large user bases, and pricing is based on Monthly Active Users (MAU), which aligns with bursty usage but may be costly for 100,000 MAU (estimated at $7,000/month at $0.07/MAU, based on B2C Essentials plan). Integration with Azure is possible via APIs, but it's not native, requiring additional configuration.
1. Microsoft Entra ID (Azure AD B2C)

Microsoft Entra ID, specifically Azure AD B2C, is designed for external identities and integrates seamlessly with Azure. It has FedRAMP High authorization, supports social logins, and offers customization via Identity Experience Framework (custom policies). It provides self-service features, role management through App Roles or groups, and auditing via Azure Monitor. Scalability is robust, handling millions of users, and pricing is highly cost-effective with the first 50,000 MAU free and additional MAU at $0.00325 (Premium P1), e.g., $162.5/month for 100,000 MAU. However, the admin interface through the Azure portal may be less intuitive for non-technical staff, and advanced features like identity proofing require custom policy development, potentially increasing implementation time.
1. Legacy Custom-Built Authentication System

A legacy custom auth solution, likely on-premises or outdated, poses significant risks. Achieving FedRAMP compliance within six months is impractical, requiring extensive development for MFA, auditing, and identity proofing, with high maintenance costs and scalability challenges. Given the timeline and security needs, this option is not recommended.
1. Other Potential CIAM Solutions

- ForgeRock/Ping Identity: Enterprise-grade, FedRAMP High authorized, but complex to deploy, especially self-hosted, and may not fit the six-month timeline or limited IT staff.

- AWS Cognito: Scalable, FedRAMP compliant in AWS GovCloud, but introduces cross-cloud complexity with Azure, with limited admin features and customization, making it less suitable.

Comparison by Key Criteria
The following table summarizes the comparison across critical areas:
| Criteria | Okta/Auth0 | Microsoft Entra ID B2C | Legacy Custom |
| Security/Compliance | FedRAMP Moderate/High, strong MFA, identity proofing integrations | FedRAMP High, robust security, custom policy for proofing | High risk, likely non-compliant |
| Ease of Implementation | Quick, turn-key, leverages existing Okta knowledge | Quick for basics, custom policies may delay for advanced needs | High risk, likely exceeds 6-month timeline |
| Admin Simplicity | User-friendly UI, good for non-technical staff | Azure portal less intuitive, may need training | Requires custom UI, high maintenance |
| Scalability/Cost | Scales well, MAU-based pricing (~$7,000/month for 100,000 MAU) | Scales well, first 50,000 MAU free, then $162.5/month for 100,000 MAU | Scalability uncertain, high dev costs |
| Integration with Azure | Via APIs, not native, additional config needed | Native, seamless with Azure services and functions | Custom integration, high effort |
Recommendation and Justification
After evaluating, Microsoft Entra ID B2C is recommended for SEAA's application. It offers:
- Cost-Effectiveness: The free tier for 50,000 MAU and low additional cost ($0.00325/MAU) align with bursty usage, potentially saving thousands compared to Okta's estimated $7,000/month for 100,000 MAU.

- Azure Integration: Native integration simplifies backend connections and monitoring, crucial for the Azure-hosted application.

- Compliance: FedRAMP High authorization meets government requirements, with guidance for NIST controls (Microsoft Entra ID FedRAMP Compliance).

- Feasibility: While custom policies may add some implementation time, the six-month timeline is achievable, especially with SEAA's Azure familiarity.

Okta/Auth0, while excellent for admin experience and identity proofing integrations (Okta Identity Proofing Integrations), is significantly more expensive, which may not justify the cost for a government application with budget constraints. The legacy custom solution is dismissed due to security and timeline risks.
Architectural Considerations
For Microsoft Entra ID B2C, SEAA should set up a tenant for external users, configure user flows for self-service registration (email/password, social logins for households), and use custom policies for identity proofing if needed. Roles can be managed via App Roles or groups, with integration to Azure functions for backend services. Audit logs can be routed to Azure Monitor for compliance reporting.
Future-Proofing
Microsoft Entra ID continues to evolve, with recent enhancements like Entra External ID, ensuring scalability and feature updates. This aligns with SEAA's long-term needs, especially for potential expansions like mobile apps.
In summary, Microsoft Entra ID B2C balances cost, security, and integration, making it the optimal choice for SEAA's scholarship application, launched by March 31, 2025, to meet all specified requirements.
Key Citations
- Configuring Okta for FedRAMP Compliance

- Microsoft Entra ID FedRAMP High Impact Configuration

- Okta vs Microsoft Entra ID IAM Comparison

- Microsoft Entra ID B2C Pricing Details

- Okta Customer Identity Cloud Pricing Overview



# Microsoft Entra ID Hub and Spoke Model for K12

*Created: 2025-06-03T20:09:20.582Z by Robert Beasy*
*Last Updated: 2025-07-21T18:15:56.342Z by Robert Beasy*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4053696597/Microsoft+Entra+ID+Hub+and+Spoke+Model+for+K12*

---

Summary of 7/11/2025 meeting
Identity Management Sources
- K12 Entra ID: Will be located in Azure Government cloud along with all the Azure resources for the K12 program.
This is the primary subject of this document.

- The source of truth for all granted permissions.

K12 B2C Entra ID: This will be located in the standard Azure cloud. This is required because Azure Government Cloud doesn't allow B2C connections.
- All
Provider Dashboard Users, 

- School Dashboard Users, 

- Parents and 

- Proxy Parents 

will gain access via the K12 B2C Entra ID tenant.
- CFI Entra ID: Is already located in the standard Azure Cloud alongside K12 development resources.

- SEAA Okta: The identity provider used by SEAA employees.

 
Trust Relationships and Authentication Flow
For a cohesive identity management framework and enable seamless authentication across the K12 program, the K12 Entra ID must establish trust relationships with the following identity providers:
1. K12 B2C Entra ID

2. CFI Entra ID

3. SEAA Okta

These trust relationships are foundational for:
- User Referencing: Enabling K12 Entra ID to recognize and reference users authenticated by these external providers.

- Login Enablement: Ensuring secure and efficient login capabilities for all users within the K12 program ecosystem.

 
 
The Hub and Spoke Architecture
 
What:
 
In this "Hub and Spoke" model Azure Entra ID is the central Identity Hub, and APIM and a Unified API Gateway Middleware method, built with C# Azure Functions, is the single, intelligent entry point to all data resources (the spokes).
 
- Azure Entra ID will serve as the central Identity Hub, where all entities and their relationships are defined using a scalable model.

- APIM and a single unified security API middleware method, referenced by all access to Azure Functions, will act as the gateway to all data. This gateway will make authorization decisions based on the identity hub and then securely broker access to the data spokes. 

- Azure SQL and Azure Data Lake Storage (ADLS) Gen2 will contain the data (spokes).

 
Why:
This approach focuses: 
- on minimal support costs by leveraging automation and delegating administration

- scalability and maintainability of an attribute-based system

- a strong defense-in-depth posture at the data layer

- auditable permissions model

 
Guiding Architectural Principles
1. Centralized Identity, Decentralized Administration: Entra ID is the single source of truth for "who is who," but daily user management is delegated to the institutions themselves.

2. Attribute-Driven Authorization: Permissions are determined by the attributes and relationships of an identity, not by managing thousands of individual roles or access lists.

3. Unified API Gateway: All applications access data through a single, secure API middleware method. 

4. centralizes security logic, making it consistent, maintainable, and easy to monitor. It contains all business and authorization logic, making it easy to update, test, and audit. 

5. No application ever accesses data directly.

6. Defense-in-Depth: While the API is the primary gatekeeper, the data stores will have their own security layer (RLS, RBAC) to prevent unauthorized access in all circumstances.

 
Step 1: Configure Azure Entra ID (the Identity Hub)
1. Object Representation

- People (Students, Parents, Schools Admins, Vendors Admins, etc.): All will be represented as User objects.
Students: Student user objects will be created with accountEnabled: false and no password. They are "non-loginable" "Identity Records" that serve as anchors for permissions. This model is free and does not consume licenses.

Institutions (Schools and Providers): Each will be an Entra ID Security Group (e.g., K12-School-LincolnHigh, K12-Vendor-Mathnasium). The staff who work at these institutions will be members of their respective groups.
 
1. Custom Security Attributes

This is the scalable and manageable way to define relationships. In Entra ID, create an Attribute Set called studentAccessControl and define the following attributes (These will be assigned to each student's user object):
- Attribute Set: studentAccessControl

- Attributes to be defined: 
parentReadWrite (String, Multi-value): Stores the Object ID(s) of the primary parent(s).

- proxyReadOnly (String, Multi-value): Stores the Object ID(s) of the proxy parent(s).

- enrolledSchoolId (String, Single-value): Stores the Object ID of the School's Security Group.

- enrolledVendorId (String, Single-value): Stores the Object ID of the Tutor's Security Group.

 
 
1. Delegated Administration: Administrative Units (AUs) (Key to Minimizing Support)

Use Administrative Units (AUs) to allow schools to manage their own staff.
This is the key to minimizing central IT workload.
1. For each school, create an Administrative Unit (e.g., "AU - Northwood High").

2. Add the school's staff User objects and the school's Security Group to this AU. [Place all of that school's staff User objects and the school's Security Group (K12-School-LincolnHigh) into this AU.]

3. Delegate Roles: 

- Assign the school's designated administrator scoped to the school's AU. This allows them to manage users and group memberships for their own school only.
This will allow school admins to reset passwords and manage group memberships for their school only, without needing to file a ticket with central IT.

 
1. Application Roles

In the App Registration for the API, we will define a small number of broad, capability-based roles
- K12.Admin

- School.Admin

- Vendor.Admin

- PrimaryParent.User

- ProxyParent.User

 
1. Groups

- For each school, an admin group will be created for it.

- For each vendor, an admin group will be created for it.

 
Summary:
- K12.Admin Role give access to be a K12 administrator.

- Vendor and School roles only give a short cut for guarding against access to all related resources.
A vendor gains access to their 

 
- unanswered question: what if a user is an admin for more than 1 vendor?

- unanswered question: what if a user is an admin for more than 1 school?

 
Parents and ProxyParents gain access to their student's resources by:
1. Being in the role of PrimaryParent or ProxyParent

2. Being granted access via custom security attributes.

 
Step 2: Configure API Management (APIM) to require JWT token access
 
Use Authorization to control what endpoints a request is allowed to call.
For example lock down access to K12.Admin endpoints to K12.Admins.
 
Step 3: Build the Unified Security Gateway (C# Azure Function middleware)
 
This API middleware is the gateway. It centralizes authorization logic.
 
1. Authorization Logic Flow

When a request is received (e.g., GET /api/students/{studentId}/registration_program):
1. Validates the user's JWT token, extracting their Object ID and their broad application role (e.g., Parent.User).

2. Makes a single, efficient Microsoft Graph API call to retrieve the target student's User object, requesting the customSecurityAttributes in the query.

3. existence of a Function Attribute(LoadAccountData) is used to load the Account from DB into function Context. if the Entra Account is not associated with a User Account the Middleware will deny access.

4. A clear, maintainable C# if/else if/else block determines access: 

The middleware business method: 
| // Assume this is a C# Azure Function middleware handling HTTP requests// User's JWT token is validated by APIM; user object contains ObjectId and roles// Resource type and ID are extracted from the request (e.g., path or params)// Main authorization functionfunction bool canAccess(User user, string resourceType, string resourceId, string operationType){    if (!user.isAuthenticated) {        return false;    }    // K12 Admins have unrestricted access to all resources    if (hasRole(user, "K12.Admin")) {        return true;    }    // Dispatch based on resource type    switch (resourceType) {        case "Enrollment":             return true;        case "Student":            return checkStudentAccess(user, resourceId, operationType);        case "School":            return checkSchoolAccess(user, resourceId, operationType);        case "Vendor":            return checkVendorAccess(user, resourceId, operationType);        case "Document":            return checkDocumentAccess(user, resourceId, operationType);        default:            return false; // Unknown resource type    }    return false;}// Check access for student resourcesfunction bool checkStudentAccess(User user, string studentId, string operationType){    // Fetch student's custom security attributes via Graph API    StudentAttributes attrs = getStudentAttributes(studentId);    if (hasRole(user, "PrimaryParent.User")) {        if (operationType == "READ") {            return attrs.parentReadWrite.Contains(user.ObjectId) ||                    attrs.proxyReadOnly.Contains(user.ObjectId);        } else if (operationType == "WRITE") {            return attrs.parentReadWrite.Contains(user.ObjectId);        }    } else if (hasRole(user, "School.Admin")) {        return isMemberOfGroup(user.ObjectId, attrs.enrolledSchoolId);    } else if (hasRole(user, "Vendor.Admin")) {        return isMemberOfGroup(user.ObjectId, attrs.enrolledVendorId);    }    return false;}// Check access for school resourcesfunction bool checkSchoolAccess(User user, string schoolId, string operationType){    // SchoolId is the security group ID (e.g., K12-School-LincolnHigh)    return hasRole(user, "School.Admin") && isMemberOfGroup(user.ObjectId, schoolId);}// Check access for vendor resourcesfunction bool checkVendorAccess(User user, string vendorId, string operationType){    // VendorId is the security group ID (e.g., K12-Vendor-Mathnasium)    return hasRole(user, "Vendor.Admin") && isMemberOfGroup(user.ObjectId, vendorId);}// Check access for document resources in ADLSfunction bool checkDocumentAccess(User user, string documentPath, string operationType){    DocumentPath parsed = parseDocumentPath(documentPath);    if (parsed.application == "enrollment") {        string schoolId = parsed.legalEntity; // e.g., lincoln-high        string studentId = parsed.userId;     // e.g., student123        // Allow if user can access the student or the school        return checkStudentAccess(user, studentId, operationType) ||                checkSchoolAccess(user, schoolId, operationType);    } else if (parsed.application == "providerdashboard") {        string vendorId = parsed.legalEntity; // e.g., math-tutor123        string employeeId = parsed.userId;    // e.g., employee123        // Allow vendor admins or the employee themselves (read-only)        return checkVendorAccess(user, vendorId, operationType) ||                (user.ObjectId == employeeId && operationType == "READ");    }    return false;}// Utility functionsfunction bool hasRole(User user, string role){    return user.roles.Contains(role);}function bool isMemberOfGroup(string userId, string groupId){    // Perform Graph API call to check membership    // Consider caching for performance    return graphApiClient.IsMemberOf(userId, groupId);}function StudentAttributes getStudentAttributes(string studentId){    // Graph API call to fetch student&rsquo;s customSecurityAttributes    return graphApiClient.GetUserAttributes(studentId, "studentAccessControl");}function DocumentPath parseDocumentPath(string path){    // Example path: /enrollment/lincoln-high/student123/apartmentleasingagreement    string[] parts = path.Split('/');    return new DocumentPath {        application = parts[1],        legalEntity = parts[2],        userId = parts[3],        documentType = parts[4]    };}// Struct to hold parsed document path componentsstruct DocumentPath {    string application;    string legalEntity;    string userId;    string documentType;} |
| --- |
 
1. Secure Data Access Methods

Once authorized, the function fetches data securely:
- Azure SQL Data: The function uses the On-Behalf-Of (OBO) flow to exchange the user's token for one scoped to Azure SQL. It then connects to the database as that user, enabling the RLS (Row-level security) defense-in-depth layer.

- PDF Files in ADLS Gen2: The function generates a short-lived (~5 minute), permission-scoped Shared Access Signature (SAS) token for the specific blob requested. It returns a URL containing this SAS token to the client, which downloads the file directly from storage. This is highly secure and performant.

 
Step 4: Implement Defense-in-Depth in Data Stores
This final layer provides failsafe security directly at the data source (protected even from potential application-level bugs).
 
1. Azure SQL: Simplified Row-Level Security (RLS)

- Note: RLS will not contain complex business logic. It will act as a simple backstop.

- A trusted automated process will sync Entra ID attributes to populate an Azure SQL table UserResourceAccessMap. This map table can be updated by a trusted, automated process that syncs from the Entra ID attributes on a timer trigger. UserResourceAccessMap will be locked down to only allowing a Managed Identity to select/insert/update/delete. Entra ID is the source of truth, the SQL table is only a projection.

- When the API connects to SQL (using the OBO flow), it first sets the caller's identity in the session context: EXEC sp_set_session_context @key=N'UserObjectId', @value=@user_object_id.

- A simple RLS predicate function will then check against the pre-populated UserResourceAccessMap table to ensure a valid relationship exists for that user and student.

Matthew C: Use SqlKata decorator to implement in the sample method.
 
1. ADLS Gen2 with Hierarchical Namespaces: Simplified RBAC and SAS

- Access is handled by assigning broad RBAC roles (e.g., Storage Blob Data Reader) to the Security Groups (e.g., K12-School-LincolnHigh) at a specific folder level. 

- Of course. Based on the detailed requirements and the relative strengths and weaknesses of different Azure services, here is the best-practice architectural approach. 

- Additionally short-lived SAS tokens generated by the API. This avoids managing millions of individual Access Control List (ACL) entries.

 
| Planned structure for easy permission management ADLS Gen2 with HNS: /{application}/{legal-entity}/{userid}/{documentType} then all documents will be stored inside documentType directories. Roughly:- application: maps to the course grained roles
- legal-entity: maps to the groups created for schools & vendors, etc.
- userid: refers to the custom security attributes
 Semi-fictional Examples: Enrollment:/enrollment/lincoln-high/student123/apartmentleasingagreement When a parent uploads a document:- Parents and parentProxy are granted access to their own uploaded document at the {userid} level.
- The school's admins have access to the document(s) at the legal-entity level.
- K12.Admin SEAA admins have access to the document(s) at either the application level.
 Provider Dashboard: /providerdashboard/math-tutor123/employee123/verificationdocument123 When a provider's employee uploads a document:- Employee could access their own document(s) at the {userid} level.
- The provider's admins have access to the document(s) at the {legal-entity} level.
- K12.Admin SEAA admins have access to the document(s) at either the application level. |

### Miyagi Do
Entra Integration Remaining work

The following code areas need to make these adjustments. 
Enrollment.cs → GetAllTasks:
Takes in an accountId. I don&rsquo;t think this function is being used anywhere so it should be able to be deleted.Schools.cs → GetSchools:
mocked out fake data to define what campuses the user had access to. It is getting removed though in the Row Level Access task (K12-2850)TaskTypeRepository.cs → CreateTaskType:
A mocked out &ldquo;var accountId = await GetWorkingAccountId();&rdquo; is used to assigned CreatedByAccountId. This will need to change to use the token&rsquo;s EntraUserObjectId coming from the ClaimAccessor TaskTypeRepository.cs → UpdateTaskType:
A mocked out &ldquo;var accountId = await GetWorkingAccountId();&rdquo; is used to assigned ModifiedByAccountId. This will need to change to use the token&rsquo;s EntraUserObjectId coming from the ClaimAccessor TaskRepository.cs → CreateTask:
AccountId, CreatedByAccountId and AssignedAdminAccountId. They all need to change to be EntraUserObjectId based. CreatedByAccountId and AssignedAdminAccountId needs to come from the ClaimAccessor which gets the EntraUserObjectId from the entra bearer token.TaskRepository.cs → UpdateTask:
AccountId, AssignedAdminAccountId and ModifiedByAccountId. They all need to change to be EntraUserObjectId based. ModifiedByAccountId needs to come from the ClaimAccessor which gets the EntraUserObjectId from the entra bearer token.### Eagle Fang
