# Rule Engine (.NET/C#)

*Created: 2025-11-04T17:10:46.988Z by Matt Vaughn*
*Last Updated: 2025-11-07T16:47:25.405Z by Matt Vaughn*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4420075531/Rule+Engine+.NET+C*

---

## I. Executive Summary: Recommendations and Architectural Fit
This analysis evaluates rules engines for implementation within the Business Logic Layer (BLL) of a high-scale, multi-application SaaS product running on an Azure backend. The selection criteria prioritize conventionality, robust community support, excellent developer experience (DX) via a Fluent API, native composability, and the critical requirement that all rule definitions must reside in C# source code, eliminating external configuration files (e.g., JSON, XML). The engine must facilitate orchestration by making determinations and proceeding with specific actions based on rule evaluations.
### 1.1. Core Finding and Architectural Priority
The stringent requirement for defining rules strictly in C# source code drives the architectural decision toward compiled, strongly-typed solutions, prioritizing reliability and testability over business user configurability.1 This immediately focuses the assessment on libraries designed around an internal Domain Specific Language (DSL) implemented via Fluent C# APIs.
### 1.2. Primary Recommendation
NRules is the definitive choice for handling complex BLL orchestration and decision-making logic.
NRules is a dedicated, open-source Rete-based inference engine http://for.NET .2 Its architecture is specifically designed for:
1. Orchestration and Actions: Explicitly supports action execution via the Then() clause, which interacts naturally with the BLL context.3

2. Complex Composability: Uses the Rete algorithm to efficiently correlate multiple facts (domain objects) to fire rules (Rule of Rules), supporting forward chaining.2

### 1.3. Secondary Recommendations and Layered Strategy
For holistic architectural integrity, the analysis suggests a layered approach employing two distinct tools based on function:
1. Input Validation (Pre-Orchestration): FluentValidation should be leveraged exclusively for ensuring data integrity and validating BLL invariants (e.g., ensuring required fields are present). Its superior Fluent API for property-level validation provides an unmatched developer experience for this specific task.4

2. Highly Controlled Policies: The Custom Rules Engine Pattern (Strategy/Specification pattern) is recommended for smaller sets of sequential policies where absolute control over execution order and zero external dependencies are paramount.

### 1.4. Overview of the Decision Matrix
The evaluation contrasts three viable approaches: the dedicated Inference Engine (NRules), the specialized Validation Framework (FluentValidation), and the flexible Custom Pattern. The critical differentiator is the native support for action execution and complex fact correlation, where NRules provides the most architecturally appropriate solution for BLL orchestration.
## II. Defining the Business Logic Layer (BLL) Rules Engine Mandate
The implementation of a rules engine within the BLL of a SaaS product requires a precise understanding of its role, distinguishing between simple data validation and complex decision orchestration.
### 2.1. Distinction: Validation vs. Decision/Orchestration BRE
The user requires the engine to "coordinate/orchestrate BL with rules to make determinations and proceed with specific 'actions'" [Query]. This maps directly to a Decision Management System (DMS), not merely a validation framework.
#### The Orchestration Requirement
A DMS focuses on complex determination&mdash;evaluating multiple interdependent facts to arrive at a business decision (e.g., premium calculation, eligibility determination) and executing consequential actions (e.g., creating a record, sending a notification).5 This decision-centric role requires a structure that explicitly facilitates defining and executing side effects or state changes. NRules supports this natively via its Then() clause.3
#### Validation's Role
Validation frameworks like FluentValidation are optimized for ensuring BLL invariants&mdash;that is, confirming the input object adheres to essential data integrity rules (e.g., Forename is not empty, Address length is acceptable).4 The core function is to produce a list of errors (ValidationResult).6
#### Architectural Misalignment
Using a validation library for core decision logic represents a functional stretch. While FluentValidation allows custom rules and access to the execution context 7, relying on Custom methods or relying on the application service layer to check the validation results and then execute side effects fundamentally blurs the line between validation and decision management.8 This approach often results in the BLL's core logic being obscured by the mechanism designed to return errors, undermining the Single Responsibility Principle. A dedicated rules engine must execute what must be done, not merely report what is wrong.
### 2.2. The Non-Negotiable Constraint: C# Source Code Only
The mandate to define rules exclusively in C# source code, explicitly prohibiting externalized configurations (e.g., JSON files) [Query], establishes a critical architectural boundary.
#### Justification: Reliability and Testability
Defining rules using an internal DSL in C# provides superior architectural reliability:
1. Type Safety: Compile-time type checking eliminates runtime failures caused by malformed rule syntax or invalid object paths, a common issue with dynamic expressions found in externalized rules.1

2. High Testability: Rules encapsulated in C# classes are inherently unit-testable and traceable, supporting the "High Testability" goal of the Rule Engine Pattern.1 Unit tests for rules can be executed alongside the rest of the application code, ensuring modularity and easier maintenance.1

#### Architectural Consequence
This constraint mandates a trade-off. While external configuration often provides greater business agility (allowing non-developers to modify rules without code deployment) 5, the user's preference for in-code rules prioritizes technical stability and developer experience (DX). This preference disqualifies popular C# rules engines such as RulesEngine (Microsoft) 11 which rely entirely on external JSON definition for rules and workflows.
## III. Candidate Rules Engines: Detailed Technical Assessment
Three viable candidates satisfy the core requirement of C# source code rule definition, offering distinct architectural models for BLL implementation.
### A. Candidate 1: NRules &ndash; The Rete Inference Engine (Primary Recommendation)
#### 3.A.1. Overview and Rete Architecture
NRules is an open-source, flexible forward-chaining rules engine based on the highly efficient Rete matching algorithm.2 The Rete algorithm is optimized to handle a large volume of facts and rules by building a network that efficiently matches new facts against existing patterns, minimizing re-evaluation and reducing rule executions.2
#### Deep Dive into Rete Optimization
The Rete algorithm operates by trading memory for speed.14 It stores the state of partial matches within its network. This is exceptionally powerful in scenarios common to SaaS BLLs, such as complex eligibility calculation or fraud detection, where decisions rely on correlating numerous interdependent facts (e.g., "Find all Active Accounts owned by a Preferred Customer").3 The inference engine dynamically figures out which rules to activate based on the inserted facts, utilizing a conflict resolution strategy, rather than executing rules in a predefined, linear order.2
#### 3.A.2. Pros and Cons for SaaS BLL Orchestration
#### 3.A.3. Rule Composition and Fluent API Example (C# Source Code)
NRules uses an internal DSL via a Fluent C# API for rule definition.17 Rules are defined as classes inheriting from Rule.
The following example demonstrates a composite rule that performs complex fact correlation&mdash;a "Rule of Rules" that only fires when both a specific type of Customer and a specific type of Account owned by that customer are present (matching two distinct facts) 3:
C#
c#(() => customer, c => c.IsPreferred)
            // 3. Match the second fact, correlating it to the first (Account is Active AND Owner matches the matched Customer)
           .Match(() => account, a => a.Owner == customer, a => a.IsActive);

        Then()
            // 4. Execute the necessary orchestration action
           .Do(ctx => customer.DoSomething()); 
    }
}]]>This structure is ideal for the required BLL orchestration, as the Then() clause explicitly executes the determination logic and subsequent actions.3
#### 3.A.4. Licensing and Community
NRules is licensed under the permissive MIT license.2 It is a conventional, well-known rules engine in the.NET ecosystem with active discussions and support available on platforms like Stack Overflow and GitHub.2
### B. Candidate 2: FluentValidation &ndash; The Fluent Validation Framework
#### 3.B.1. Overview and Use Case
FluentValidation is a robust and widely adopted.NET library specifically designed for building strongly-typed object validation rules.4 It is celebrated for its highly readable and intuitive Fluent API.4
#### Architectural Limitation: Pseudo-BRE Role
While FluentValidation satisfies the requirement for in-code rule definition and provides an exceptional DX, it is fundamentally optimized for validation, not orchestration.8 If utilized as a decision engine, it must be heavily adapted, requiring developers to implement side effects by relying on Custom rule extensions, or by injecting service dependencies into the validator to trigger actions.7 This approach essentially shoehorns business actions into a framework primarily designed to generate error messages, increasing complexity and reducing clarity compared to a dedicated BRE.
#### 3.B.2. Pros and Cons as a Pseudo-BRE
| Aspect | Evaluation |
| Pros | Best-in-class Fluent API.4 Extremely high testability for individual validation rules. Massive community support.20 Seamless DI integration.4 Highly performant for linear property checking.21 |
| Cons | Execution is sequential/linear, not based on inference. Poor native support for action/orchestration execution.9 Primarily property-centric logic.4 Architectural risk when used for complex, state-changing logic.8 |
#### 3.B.3. Rule Composition and Fluent API Example (C# Source Code)
FluentValidation supports composition through conditional logic (When/Unless) 9 and RuleSets, allowing rules to be grouped and executed selectively.22
C#
c# { 
    public PersonValidator() { 
        // Composition via RuleSet for grouping related logic:
        RuleSet("Names", () => { 
            RuleFor(x => x.Surname).NotNull(); 
            RuleFor(x => x.Forename).NotNull(); 
        }); 
        
        // Conditional rule execution:
        RuleFor(customer => customer.CustomerDiscount)
           .GreaterThan(0)
           .When(customer => customer.IsPreferredCustomer); 
    } 
}]]>#### 3.B.4. Licensing and Community
FluentValidation is licensed under Apache 2. The project maintainers explicitly request financial sponsorship for commercial use, highlighting the importance of supporting the ongoing development of this highly valued tool.4
### C. Candidate 3: Custom Rule Engine Pattern (The Fully Controlled Approach)
#### 3.C.1. Overview (Strategy/Specification Pattern Implementation)
The Custom Rules Engine Pattern involves creating an internal architecture leveraging established design patterns, such as the Strategy Pattern for individual rules (IRule) and the Chain of Responsibility or Specification Pattern for sequencing and aggregation.23
#### Architectural Merit for Sequential Policies
This approach offers maximum control and testability. For a SaaS platform where certain rule sets are relatively small (e.g., fewer than 50 rules) and the logic is inherently sequential (e.g., applying additive discounts or checking a strict sequence of policies), a hand-rolled engine often avoids the complexity and dependency overhead of a full inference engine like NRules.25 Every rule is a standalone, testable class, maximizing adherence to the Single Responsibility Principle (SRP).1
#### 3.C.2. Pros and Cons
| Aspect | Evaluation |
| Pros | Ultimate control over sequencing, performance, and context. Highest testability (rules are isolated C# classes). Zero third-party dependencies for the core logic. Naturally integrates action execution via the Command pattern.23 |
| Cons | High initial boilerplate required to set up rule discovery, aggregation, and the execution engine.24 Maintenance burden for core engine features (e.g., conflict resolution, dependency management) must be handled internally. |
#### 3.C.3. Composition and Action Example (C# Source Code)
The pattern typically defines a rule interface and an engine that manages the execution flow:
C#
c# _rules;
    
    public DiscountRuleEngine(IEnumerable rules)
    {
        _rules = rules; // Rules are injected via DI
    }

    // Engine iterates and applies rules sequentially (Chain of Responsibility)
    public decimal CalculateDiscountPercentage(Customer customer)
    {
        decimal discount = 0m;
        foreach(var rule in _rules)
        {
            // Applies the rule and aggregates the result (Math.Max for maximizing discount)
            discount = Math.Max(discount, rule.CalculateDiscount(customer, discount)); 
        }
        return discount;
    }
}]]>### D. Candidate 4: RulesEngine (Microsoft) &ndash; External Configuration Anti-Pattern (Benchmark Only)
RulesEngine is a http://powerful.NET  library built for abstracting business logic using C# expressions defined in JSON workflows.11 While it offers extensibility, including custom action support 11, its explicit reliance on JSON definition for rules immediately violates the user's primary architectural constraint: no external configuration files [Query]. This reliance sacrifices type safety and compile-time testability for dynamic agility, making it unsuitable for this selection process.
## IV. Comparative Analysis and Decision Matrix
To provide a clear, justified selection, the candidates are compared against the critical requirements established for BLL orchestration.
Table 1: Technical Due Diligence Comparison Grid
| Attribute/Feature | NRules (Rete Inference Engine) | FluentValidation (Validation Library) | Custom Rule Pattern (Strategy/Specification) |
| Primary Use Case | Complex Decision Making, Orchestration, State Inference | Strongly-Typed Object/Input Validation | Decoupled Policy Execution, Sequential Logic |
| Rule Definition Mechanism | C# Internal DSL / Fluent API 17 | C# Fluent API 4 | C# Interfaces / Classes 24 |
| Developer Experience (Fluent API) | Excellent (Pattern matching on facts) | Exceptional (Cleanest DSL for property assertion) | High (Standard C# classes, familiar patterns) |
| Composite Rules (Rule of Rules) | Highest (Rete Network for complex fact correlation) 3 | Good (RuleSets, When/Otherwise) 22 | Excellent (Customizable Chain of Responsibility/Aggregation) |
| Action/Orchestration Support | Native via Then() block; supports forward chaining.3 | Implicit (Requires Custom or external service layer logic).7 | Native (Rule class contains determination and action logic).24 |
| Testability | High (Rules are isolated C# classes) 1 | Highest (Designed for Unit Testing Validators) 4 | Highest (Atomic rules, simple mocking) 1 |
| Execution Model | Rete Algorithm (Optimized for dynamic matching) 2 | Linear execution against properties | Customizable (Linear, Chain of Responsibility) 23 |
### BLL Action Trigger and Execution Model Mapping
The success of a BLL rules engine hinges on its ability to transition seamlessly from rule determination to action execution. This distinguishes true orchestration tools from pure validation utilities.
Table 2: BLL Action Trigger and Execution Model Mapping
| Engine | Rule Execution Trigger | Mechanism for Action Execution | Suitability for Orchestration |
| NRules | ISession.Fire() based on inserted Facts. | Then() block, which executes C# code (commands or state changes) directly within the rules context. | High (Inference leads directly to action execution and possible subsequent rule firing). |
| FluentValidation | IValidator.Validate(object) call. | External logic checks the ValidationResult; or complex custom rules must be written to inject side effects. | Low (Designed to fail early and return error state, not execute core domain actions). |
| Custom Rule Pattern | Engine.Execute(context) call. | Dedicated methods (e.g., IRule.ApplyAction) that receive the context and execute a Command.23 | High (Provides tailored, type-safe control over sequential logic and required side effects). |
## V. Deep Dive: Implementation, Integration, and Developer Experience
### 5.1. Fluent API Deep Dive and Expressiveness
The requested Fluent API provides syntactic sugar that significantly enhances developer experience.19
- NRules Expressiveness: The NRules DSL is highly expressive for complex logical patterns. It shifts focus from property-level constraints to fact correlation: defining what state must exist across multiple domain objects simultaneously. For example, the When().Match<T>() syntax explicitly describes the desired state pattern that must be present in the working memory for the Then() action to fire.3

- FluentValidation Expressiveness: FluentValidation's DSL is peerless for defining constraints on a single object's properties (e.g., RuleFor(x => x.Prop).GreaterThan(0)). This is ideal for initial input data checks but lacks the inherent structural tools for expressing dynamic relationships between disparate domain entities needed for complex BLL orchestration.4

### 5.2. Rule Testability Frameworks
The mandate for in-code rules means that testability is maximized across all candidates. Each engine allows individual rules to be tested in isolation, adhering to the Rule Engine Pattern's core benefit of high test coverage.1
- NRules Testing Strategy: NRules testing involves setting up an in-memory ISessionFactory, creating an ISession, inserting the required facts (domain objects) into the session, calling session.Fire(), and then asserting on the resultant state changes or actions performed by the Then() clause.18

- FluentValidation Testing Strategy: Testing relies on calling the Validate() method and asserting that the resulting ValidationResult contains the expected success or error messages.6 Test extensions are often utilized to simplify this process.4

- Custom Pattern Testing Strategy: Individual rule classes (IRule) are tested as pure functions or commands, maximizing the simplicity of mock dependencies and ensuring each rule strictly follows the SRP.1

### 5.3. Handling Rule Actions (The Core Orchestration Mechanism)
For orchestration, the BLL requires definitive action execution&mdash;the crucial distinction from pure validation.
- NRules Action Integration: The Then() clause is the native mechanism for executing actions. It receives the IContext, allowing the rule to access services injected via DI, modify existing facts, or insert new facts (forward chaining).3 This integrated, session-aware mechanism is architecturally clean for complex state transitions.

- FluentValidation Action Workaround: As noted, FluentValidation requires using methods like Custom to execute actions (side effects) within the validation pipeline.7 This forces domain actions to be implemented inside a validation pipeline, which complicates debugging and muddies the framework&rsquo;s intent, confirming its primary role is to prevent invalid state, not to execute core decision logic.

## VI. Architectural and Operational Considerations for Azure SaaS
Implementing any rules engine in a scalable.NET/C# Azure backend requires specific attention to performance, state management, and integration with the cloud environment.
### 6.1. Performance and Scalability in Azure
#### Rete Performance Model
The Rete algorithm in NRules provides significant performance advantages when the rules involve complex pattern matching and correlation across many facts.14 This is because the Rete network efficiently determines which rules are applicable without re-evaluating every condition, making it highly suitable for high-throughput, intricate decision flows characteristic of a scaling SaaS platform (e.g., calculating a complex fee structure for millions of transactions).
However, the Rete network creation and initial memory overhead might be unnecessary for simple, sequential policy checks. In such cases, the linear execution of a Custom Rule Pattern or FluentValidation would likely maintain simpler, predictable latency.
#### Azure Scaling and Session Management
Azure scaling demands horizontal scalability, requiring services to be largely stateless. NRules must be integrated carefully: the ISessionFactory (which holds the compiled Rete network) must be treated as a thread-safe singleton, created once during application startup. Per-request decision making requires a short-lived ISession instance to be created from the factory, facts inserted, rules fired, and the session discarded immediately after the transaction.15 This pattern ensures the BLL logic remains horizontally scalable across multiple Azure App Service instances.
### 6.2. Dependency Management and IoC Integration
Robust DI integration is essential for allowing rules to interact with the broader BLL, such as accessing data repositories or invoking external services.
- NRules Integration: NRules offers specific extension points to handle dependency resolution. The RuleRepository relies on IRuleActivator to instantiate rule classes. By integrating with http://standard.NET  DI containers (e.g., using integration packages for Autofac), services can be seamlessly injected directly into the rule classes, enabling complex logic that pulls external data or performs transactional operations.15

- FluentValidation/Custom Integration: These candidates naturally support DI integration, as the rules (validators or IRule classes) are standard C# services resolved and injected via the application&rsquo;s IoC container.

### 6.3. Future-Proofing and Maintenance
The choice of an in-code definition fundamentally binds rule changes to the software development lifecycle (SDLC), requiring code review, compilation, and deployment for updates. This aligns perfectly with the user's priority for high governance and stability.
- NRules Maintenance: Rule logic changes within the NRules C# classes necessitate recompilation. The engine&rsquo;s canonical rule model, however, ensures structured and robust rule loading, offering internal extensibility and clear separation between the fluent DSL definition and the runtime network.17

- Custom Pattern Maintenance: This pattern maximizes adherence to the Open/Closed Principle. Adding a new rule simply requires adding a new class implementing the shared interface, minimizing modifications to existing code.24

## VII. Conclusion and Final Recommendation
The architectural assessment confirms that NRules is the optimal rules engine for the SaaS product&rsquo;s BLL, specifically meeting the critical requirements for in-code definition, strong Fluent API, and native support for orchestration actions.
The following layered implementation strategy is recommended to leverage the strengths of the top candidates while maintaining architectural clarity:
1. Input Validation (Pre-processing): Utilize FluentValidation at the service boundary to validate Data Transfer Objects (DTOs) and ensure data integrity invariants. This maximizes developer productivity for simple constraints.

2. Core Decision Orchestration: Implement complex decision-making, correlation, and action execution using NRules. The Rete algorithm provides the necessary performance advantage and composition capability for high-volume, interdependent business logic required by a scaling SaaS platform. The use of the Then() clause for actions ensures clean architectural separation between determination and effect.

3. Sequential Policy Execution (Optional Optimization): Reserve the Custom Rules Engine Pattern for policy sets that are simple, strictly sequential, and where dependency on external data or logic is minimal. This provides maximum test control and avoids the Rete overhead where complex inference is not required.

By adopting NRules for complex decision management, the BLL gains a powerful, type-safe, and highly testable framework capable of supporting the coordination and action-based determination mandated by the SaaS product requirements.

# Handling Rule Outcomes in NRules: Logging, Auditing, and Custom Messaging

*Created: 2025-11-07T16:48:17.076Z by Matt Vaughn*
*Last Updated: 2025-11-07T16:51:53.798Z by Matt Vaughn*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4438818822/Handling+Rule+Outcomes+in+NRules+Logging+Auditing+and+Custom+Messaging*

---

The management of rule execution outcomes in a declarative rules engine like NRules requires an advanced architectural approach that correctly separates declarative business logic from application-level concerns such as logging, auditing, and user interface communication. NRules provides distinct, highly effective mechanisms to handle these requirements, primarily through the Execution Event Model for passive monitoring (Logging and Auditing) and the Result Fact Assertion Pattern coupled with Rule Metadata for active result handling (Custom Actions and User Messaging).
## I. Architectural Foundations: Managing Rule Outcomes in NRules
A foundational understanding of the NRules execution model is essential. The engine operates on the principle of successful execution: a rule successfully matches its conditions (Left-Hand Side, or LHS) and successfully executes its actions (Right-Hand Side, or RHS).1 In the context of business validation, a "failure" state (e.g., a customer data mismatch) is not an engine error; it is an application-defined state that is identified and asserted by a successfully executing rule.
### A. The Conceptual Model: Rule Firing vs. Application Failure
In rule-based systems, it is crucial to maintain a strict separation of concerns. The rule&rsquo;s definition, written using the Fluent C# DSL, specifies what state in the working memory triggers an action.2 The action itself, defined in the Then() block, should focus solely on modifying the session state or performing immediate, non-blocking operations via the context (IRuleContext). External actions&mdash;such as committing an audit record, writing to a log, or displaying a message to a user&mdash;must be handled by the host application layer, triggered by the rule's activity.
The declarative nature of the NRules framework means that rules should identify required actions (such as calculating a surcharge 1 or identifying a policy violation) rather than attempting to execute complex I/O operations. This separation ensures that the rules repository remains highly focused on volatile business logic while the underlying domain model remains stable.3
### B. The Two Pillars of Result Handling: Passive Events vs. Active Facts
NRules addresses all post-execution requirements through two primary, complementary mechanisms:
1. Passive Monitoring (The Execution Event Model): This approach is used for diagnostics, performance metrics, and compliance logging. The engine exposes lifecycle events, allowing the host application to non-intrusively observe execution flow, including facts being inserted, updated, and rules being activated or fired.4 Since this approach merely observes the engine, it is ideal for non-blocking operations like operational logging and asynchronous auditing.

2. Active Reporting (The Result Fact Assertion Pattern): This mechanism is mandatory when the rule's purpose is to communicate an actionable result back to the host application (e.g., a required calculation, a successful outcome, or a validation failure). The rule achieves this by asserting a specialized object&mdash;a Result Fact&mdash;into the working memory during its Then() action.3 After the rules engine completes its execution cycle (session.Fire()), the host application queries the session memory to retrieve these Result Facts and uses them to determine the next steps, such as displaying messages or blocking a transaction.

The following table summarizes how these two architectural pillars satisfy the requirements for managing rule outcomes:
Result Handling Patterns Based on User Requirements
| User Requirement | NRules Mechanism | Implementation Pattern | Key Integration Point |
| 1. Operational Logging | Execution Event Model (Passive) | Asynchronous handler translates RuleFiredEvent to structured log format. | ISession.Events.RuleFiredEvent |
| 1. Audit Logging | Execution Event Model (Passive) | Asynchronous queueing of event data for durable, forensic storage, leveraging Rule Name. | ISession.Events.RuleFiredEvent |
| 1. Action / User Display | Result Fact Assertion (Active) | Rule Then() action asserts a structured ValidationError fact. | IRuleContext.Insert() |
| 1. Custom Messages | Rule Metadata | DescriptionAttribute is retrieved via IRuleContext and injected into the Result Fact. | Rule Class Attributes |
## II. Requirement 3: Active Result Handling via Result Facts (User Display and Actions)
When a rule successfully identifies a condition that requires intervention, such as a validation failure or the calculation of a critical output, the prescribed NRules pattern is to assert a dedicated result fact into the session's working memory.3 This pattern is critical for handling actions and displaying messages to the user post-execution.
### A. Defining the Result Fact Model
For validation and messaging scenarios, a structured domain object, such as a ValidationError or RuleResult, must be defined. This object serves as the carrier for the message and context necessary for the application layer. A robust Result Fact must contain more than just the message; it requires sufficient context to link the result back to the source data and the source rule. Key fields typically include:
- SourceFact: A reference or identifier of the specific domain object (the input data) that triggered the rule.

- RuleName: The stable, technical name of the firing rule, used for internal lookup and auditing.2

- Severity: An enumeration (e.g., Error, Warning, Information) that guides the application on the appropriate response (e.g., block submission vs. permissive display).

- UserMessage: The custom, human-readable text that is ultimately displayed to the user.

By creating a strong contract for the Result Fact, the rule logic remains clean, and the consuming application gains a standardized output regardless of which specific rule fired.
### B. Implementing the Assertion in the Then() Clause
The assertion of the Result Fact occurs within the rule's action block. The Then() block receives the IRuleContext (ctx), which provides access to the engine's functionality, including insertion into working memory.1
The standard pattern involves matching an object in the When() clause and then using the matched object to construct the error fact in the Then() clause, followed by insertion 3:
C#
c#(() => match, x =>!x.ValidationCondition); // Condition fails
Then()
   .Do(ctx => ctx.Insert(new ValidationError(match, "Custom Message"))); // Assert Result Fact]]>A sophisticated implementation retrieves the custom user message directly from the rule's metadata, rather than hardcoding the string in the action block. This is achieved by having the Then() action access the IRuleContext to dynamically read the description attribute of the firing rule, injecting this externally defined, configurable message into the ValidationError fact before calling ctx.Insert(). This architecture ensures that the message is consistent, centralized, and satisfies the requirement for defining custom messages, which is discussed fully in Section V.
### C. Retrieving and Utilizing Rule Outcomes
After the domain facts have been inserted and the session.Fire() command is executed, the session memory holds all of the asserted Result Facts, ready for retrieval by the host application.
The mechanism for retrieval is the ISession.Query<T>() method.5 This method allows the application to pull all facts of a specified type (e.g., ValidationError) from the session memory.
C#
c#().ToList();

if (errors.Any())
{
    // Display messages to the user using the.UserMessage property
    // Prevent object construction or transaction commit
}
else
{
    // Proceed with valid object construction or commit
}]]>This declarative retrieval pattern is highly efficient and scalable. The application layer consumes the aggregated list of results, handles aggregation (in cases where multiple rules fire against the same input), and implements the action routing logic necessary to finalize the transaction or display appropriate messages based on the extracted Severity and UserMessage.
## III. Requirement 1: Operational Logging for Diagnostics and Debugging
Operational logging focuses on monitoring the execution flow for diagnostic purposes, performance tuning, and immediate troubleshooting by technical staff. This relies entirely on the NRules Execution Event Model (passive monitoring).
### A. Subscribing to Essential Execution Events
The ISession interface exposes an Events property that provides access to the IEventProvider. Subscribing to events from this provider allows the host application to hook into various points of the engine&rsquo;s lifecycle.4
For core operational logging, the most critical event is the RuleFiredEvent.1 This event is raised every time a rule's conditions are met and its actions are successfully executed. The event arguments contain essential information, including the technical name of the rule (args.Rule.Name) and the set of facts that matched the rule's conditions.
For deeper diagnostics and troubleshooting, especially in complex environments involving forward chaining or iterative rule execution, developers may also subscribe to working memory events such as FactInsertedEvent or FactUpdatedEvent.4 These events provide granular visibility into the Rete network&rsquo;s state propagation, which is invaluable for answering the question: "Why did this rule not fire?"
### B. Strategy for Integration with Structured Logging
NRules maintains a strict decoupling from external logging frameworks; it does not directly integrate http://with.NET  logging systems like Serilog, NLog, or Application Insights.4 Therefore, the responsibility falls to the application architecture to implement a translation layer.
The necessary pattern is to create dedicated event handlers that receive the NRules event arguments (e.g., RuleFiredEventArgs) and translate the relevant properties into a structured logging format. This ensures that technical details such as Rule Name, the time of execution, and key identifiers of the matched facts are captured as searchable properties in the log payload (e.g., JSON). Structured logging is vital for operations teams to efficiently query and analyze execution history across large volumes of transactions.
### C. Performance and Tiered Logging Architecture
When designing the operational logging mechanism, performance constraints must be rigorously enforced. While NRules offers detailed diagnostic capabilities, high-detail tracing functionalities carry a significant performance cost.
Analysis of similar Rete-based rule engine architectures explicitly warns that enabling capabilities such as capturing rule values or generating full execution traces significantly degrades performance and is unsuitable for production environments.6 Capturing comprehensive execution counts or detailed rule values increases both CPU overhead and memory consumption, potentially crippling a high-throughput system.
To mitigate this, an expert solution mandates a tiered logging architecture that can be dynamically controlled via configuration:
1. Level 1 (Production/Audit): This level captures minimal data, focusing only on the high-level RuleFiredEvent and essential context (Rule Name, Fact IDs). The overhead is low, making it suitable for continuous operation and basic compliance logging.

2. Level 2 (Diagnostic/Development): This level enables deep working memory tracing and Rule Values capture. It is strictly reserved for staging, quality assurance, or specific developer troubleshooting sessions.

By configuring the application layer to toggle the subscription depth based on the environment, the development team retains powerful diagnostic capabilities without introducing crippling bottlenecks in a live production environment.
## IV. Requirement 2: Persistent Audit Logging and Compliance Tracking
Audit logging demands a focus on immutability, integrity, and durability, often driven by regulatory requirements. While it shares the same event source as operational logging (RuleFiredEvent), the architectural implementation requires greater rigor.
### A. Defining the Immutable Audit Data Model
The audit record must capture not just that a rule fired, but all the necessary context for forensic reconstruction. Key requirements for the persistent audit data model include:
- Rule Context: The stable, technical identifier of the rule (derived from the NameAttribute) and any relevant tags.2

- Temporal Context: Accurate, immutable timestamps of execution.

- User/System Context: Information on the actor (user ID, system process) that initiated the session (typically injected by the application layer).

- Fact State Snapshot: The most crucial element&mdash;a serialized snapshot of the domain objects (facts) that satisfied the rule's conditions.

### B. The Asynchronous Auditing Pattern
Integrating synchronous database writes directly into the RuleFiredEvent handler is strongly discouraged. Performing I/O operations (such as SQL inserts) within the engine's event loop will block the engine's thread, significantly increasing transaction latency and jeopardizing throughput.
The expert solution employs an asynchronous auditing pattern. The RuleFiredEvent handler should only perform non-blocking actions: constructing a standardized audit payload from the event arguments and immediately queuing this payload into a decoupled message queue (e.g., Kafka, RabbitMQ, or a durable internal queue). A separate, independent service or thread then consumes these queued messages and handles the eventual durable persistence into the audit log database. This architecture ensures high-integrity auditing without compromising the low-latency requirement of the rules engine execution.
### C. Capturing Fact State for Forensic Auditing
For forensic auditing, merely logging the rule name is insufficient; the exact state of the input data that triggered the rule must be recorded. The RuleFiredEventArgs provides access to the set of matched facts. The application must extract and serialize these domain objects immediately upon receiving the RuleFiredEvent.
This proactive snapshotting is essential because the rule&rsquo;s Then() actions may immediately modify the state of the domain facts (e.g., updating a Quote object 1 or marking a fact for retraction). By capturing the state from the event arguments before these actions complete, the system guarantees an accurate forensic record of the preconditions that led to the rule's successful execution.
## V. Requirement 4: Defining and Leveraging Custom Rule Messages (Metadata Implementation)
The ability to define custom rule messages is critical for separating the technical implementation of the rule from the user-facing text, allowing business analysts to manage messaging without requiring code changes to the rule&rsquo;s core logic. NRules handles this requirement through the use of Rule Metadata attributes.
### A. Applying Rule Metadata Attributes
When defining rules using the NRules Fluent C# DSL, the rule class (which inherits from NRules.Fluent.Dsl.Rule) can be decorated with specific attributes that store metadata.2 This metadata is accessible at runtime and provides stable contextual information.
The two most important attributes for result handling and custom messaging are NameAttribute and DescriptionAttribute.2
- NameAttribute: Used to assign a stable, technical identifier to the rule. This name is indispensable for operational logging and audit record linking.

- DescriptionAttribute: The designated location for storing the custom, human-readable message intended for the end-user or operational staff.

For example, a rule applying a surcharge might be defined as:
C#
The use of TagAttribute 2 further enhances management by allowing rules to be grouped, filtered, or excluded during the rule loading process (RuleRepository.Load) 8, providing granular control over which rule sets are active in specific environments.
### B. Programmatic Retrieval of Metadata in Execution Context
The full value of the metadata is realized when it is accessed programmatically during the rule&rsquo;s execution. Within the Then() clause, the IRuleContext provides access to the firing rule&rsquo;s definition, allowing the action to retrieve the metadata defined by the attributes.
This is the critical step that connects the external message definition (Requirement 4) with the active result handling pattern (Requirement 3). Instead of asserting a hardcoded message, the rule dynamically retrieves the DescriptionAttribute value and injects it into the ValidationError object being created and inserted into the session memory.3 This pattern guarantees that if a business analyst updates the message in the rule definition, the application automatically displays the new message upon the next execution without modification to the core application logic responsible for message display.
### C. Summary of Rule Metadata Attributes
The following table summarizes the purpose of the primary metadata attributes in the context of compliance and result handling:
NRules Fluent DSL Metadata for Context and Messaging
| Attribute Name | Purpose in Result Handling | Availability Context | Requirement Satisfaction |
| NameAttribute | Technical identifier for logging, auditing, and indexing | IRuleContext, RuleFiredEvent arguments | Technical Auditing ID |
| DescriptionAttribute | Storage for the custom, human-readable user message | IRuleContext (for injection into Result Fact) | Custom User Message (Requirement 4) |
| TagAttribute | Rule classification (e.g., "Critical", "Compliance") | Rule Repository Loading, IRuleContext | Filtering, Grouping, Configurable Loading |
| PriorityAttribute | Determines the execution order among activated rules | Engine Execution Cycle | Controlling sequential validation/calculation flow |
## VI. Conclusion and Advanced Scalability Considerations
Handling rule outcomes in NRules requires a clear architectural separation between passive monitoring and active result delivery. NRules successfully manages all requirements&mdash;logging, auditing, custom actions, and messaging&mdash;through native mechanisms designed for enterprise-level integration.
Operational logging and auditing (Requirements 1 and 2) are satisfied by subscribing to the non-intrusive Execution Event Model, particularly the RuleFiredEvent. For large-scale deployments, architectural best practices dictate asynchronous queuing of audit events to prevent the rules engine from blocking on I/O operations, thereby maintaining high performance. Furthermore, diagnostic capabilities requiring high-detail tracing must be strictly isolated to non-production environments to avoid crippling the execution speed due to the overhead associated with capturing Rete network statistics and rule values.6
Active result handling and user messaging (Requirements 3 and 4) are managed by the Result Fact Assertion Pattern. Rules identify a necessary action (e.g., a validation error) and assert a structured Result Fact (ValidationError) using IRuleContext.Insert().3 Custom messages are decoupled from the rule logic by utilizing the DescriptionAttribute metadata, which is dynamically retrieved within the rule's Then() block and injected into the Result Fact. The host application then uses ISession.Query<T>() 5 post-execution to retrieve these aggregated results for display or transactional decision-making.
By adhering to these prescribed patterns&mdash;leveraging events for passive observation and result facts/metadata for active communication&mdash;organizations can build highly robust, auditable, and maintainable systems that effectively utilize the NRules engine for mission-critical business logic execution. For advanced performance analysis and optimization in complex, high-throughput scenarios, developers should utilize the NRules Metrics provider, which exposes granular performance information essential for profiling the underlying Rete network.4
#### Works cited
1. Getting Started - NRules, accessed November 7, 2025, https://nrules.net/articles/getting-started.html 

2. Fluent Rules DSL - NRules, accessed November 7, 2025, https://nrules.net/articles/fluent-rules-dsl.html 

3. How to create a validation rule with NRules? - Stack Overflow, accessed November 7, 2025, https://stackoverflow.com/questions/32554222/how-to-create-a-validation-rule-with-nrules 

4. Diagnostics - NRules, accessed November 7, 2025, https://nrules.net/articles/diagnostics.html 

5. Is it possible to retrieve facts from the session once it ran - Google Groups, accessed November 7, 2025, https://groups.google.com/g/nrules-users/c/uW5jbWfVDzI 

6. Rule Engine Execution Log | InRule, accessed November 7, 2025, https://support.inrule.com/hc/en-us/articles/4410093325453-Rule-Engine-Execution-Log 

7. Class Rule | NRules, accessed November 7, 2025, https://nrules.net/api/NRules.Fluent.Dsl.Rule.html 

8. Fluent Rules Loading - NRules, accessed November 7, 2025, https://nrules.net/articles/fluent-rules-loading.html



# Rule Engine Selection Analysis

*Created: 2025-11-04T17:11:51.159Z by Matt Vaughn*
*Last Updated: 2025-11-07T16:53:02.284Z by Matt Vaughn*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4420403213/Rule+Engine+Selection+Analysis*

---

This analysis outlines the required rule engine selection process for the K12 Scholarship modernization project, focusing on established, conventional .NET/C# solutions suitable for complex monetary, compliance, and statutory logic.
The K12 project necessitates a robust Rule Engine to function as a Policy Service or Domain Service within the Domain Layer. This service is critical for enforcing key financial invariants, such as balance constraints ($\leq$ $30,000$) on the ScholarshipAccount aggregate, rigorously validating PurchaseRequest items against allowable expense lists, and executing complex Income Calculation Rules for Award Tier determination. The goal is Policy Externalization to enhance testability, consistency, and separation from core business entities.
### ⚠️ A Note on External Information
The provided source documents detail the architectural necessity and specific rule use cases for the K12 project (e.g., Rollover Eligibility, Allowable Expenses, Income Calculation).
Therefore, the following list of candidates, features, pros/cons, and the final recommendation are based on general industry knowledge and external research relevant to the .NET ecosystem, and must be verified independently by our K12 Project Team and Platform Architecture group.
## Objective Review and Feature Comparison Checklist
The following table compares five top-rated and conventional rule engine options commonly used in .NET/C# projects, focusing on those that allow rules to be implemented in code or through code-driven configuration, avoiding low/no-code platforms.
## Pros/Cons, Merits, and Developer Experience
### 1. FluentValidation
A standard library in the .NET ecosystem, traditionally used for validating command and DTO integrity before processing.
| Category | Description | Special Capabilities / Developer Experience |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pros | Widely adopted, highly conventional, strong community support. Excellent integration with http://ASP.NET  Core and DI containers. Rules are tightly coupled to the C# object/model. | Fluent API Syntax: Extremely readable syntax (e.g., RuleFor(x => x.TotalSpent).GreaterThanOrEqualTo(1000);). Allows asynchronous validation and custom dependency injection into validators. |
| Cons | Less suited for complex, branching workflow rules (like multi-step decision tables or state transitions). Rules are code-embedded, making dynamic updates harder than JSON-based engines. | Merits for K12: Ideal for enforcing simple invariants on Aggregates like ScholarshipAccount (e.g., Minimum Spend Requirement $\geq$ $1,000$) or validating required fields on a PurchaseRequest. |
### 2. RulesEngine (Microsoft related OSS)
Designed for complex policy and business rule execution, often using declarative JSON definitions, though these definitions can be embedded or dynamically loaded.
| Category | Description | Special Capabilities / Developer Experience |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pros | Excellent for complex decision trees and rule chaining (workflows). Rules can be defined in structured formats (JSON/YAML), offering good separation of policy logic from core code. | Decision Table Support: Highly effective for mapping income/household size to Award Tier Determination. Supports rule execution in parallel and filtering/chaining logic. |
| Cons | Rules are often defined outside native C# code, potentially slowing down development iteration compared to pure C# code. Requires setting up strong type mapping for input models. | Merits for K12: Best suited for high-complexity, frequently changing, statutory rules like Prohibited Items/Allowable Categories or complex Educational Technology Constraints (30-day window, 3-year frequency limits). |
### 3. NCalc
A lightweight library focused purely on evaluating mathematical expressions and basic logic defined as strings at runtime.
| Category | Description | Special Capabilities / Developer Experience |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pros | Very fast and minimal dependency footprint. Perfect for purely computational rules that rely on complex arithmetic and Boolean logic. | Dynamic Calculation: Excellent for processing inputs like gross wages, exclusions (Combat Pay, foster payments), and net self-employment income to determine the final household income for the OS Income Calculation Model. Supports custom functions and parameters. |
| Cons | Limited support for complex external object manipulation or integration with custom domain services (e.g., calling an IsProviderEnrolled(id) method is harder). | Merits for K12: Highly effective where the rule is primarily a formula, such as the Rollover Eligibility calculation, ensuring the balance $\leq$ cumulative cap ($$30,000$). |
## Selection Criteria for the K12 Team
Given the architectural constraints and the highly sensitive, compliance-driven nature of the K12 system, the team should use the following criteria for making a final selection:
1. Complexity Handling (Calculation vs. Policy): Can the engine effectively manage both simple binary validation (e.g., Provider Enrollment Status == enrolled) and highly complex, statutory calculation models (e.g., the specific Income Calculation Model)?

2. Architectural Fit (Policy Service): How easily can the rule engine be integrated into the Domain Layer as a decoupled Policy Service, ensuring that the rules are testable and consistent across all workflows?

3. Developer Experience (Readability and Maintainability): Since the rules will be complex and subject to policy change, the syntax must be highly readable and maintainable (e.g., offering a Fluent API Syntax or a structured definition format).

4. Testability: Rules governing award amounts and monetary compliance must be easily isolated and unit-tested without relying on external dependencies.

5. Performance on Transactional Paths: For high-frequency operations like PurchaseRequest validation and debiting the ScholarshipAccount, the engine must offer reliable, low-latency performance.



# Audit Logs

*Created: 2025-11-05T21:26:54.713Z by Matt Vaughn*
*Last Updated: 2025-11-05T21:27:32.375Z by Matt Vaughn*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4429611013/Audit+Logs*

---

Based on the provided documentation regarding the Information Security Policy standards for System & Event Auditing, here is a summary of the K12&rsquo;s requirements for audit logs, covering content, event requirements, controls, and storage/retention policies.
## Summary of Audit Log Requirements
The audit log system is an integral part of CFI's security controls and must provide in-depth information regarding application processing and infrastructure issues. The goal is for the audit record to contain sufficient information to establish what occurred, when it occurred, where it occurred, the source and outcome of the event, and the identity of the trigger.
### 1. Audit Log Content Requirements
Audit records must contain the following fields:
| Content Field | Required Information |
| --- | --- |
| When it Occurred | Date and Time Stamp. Systems must log time at least down to the second, preferably down to the millisecond. Systems must use internal clocks to generate time stamps and must use either Coordinated Universal Time (UTC) or the local time offset. |
| What Occurred | Event failure, event success, or event-specific result. |
| Where it Occurred | Host name, Source and Destination IP address, etc.. |
| Who or What Triggered the Event | User ID, Process ID, Program Name, Batch/Job Name, etc.. |
| Event Description | Error codes, description fields, function being performed. |
| Event Outcome | Files or objects accessed or modified, changes made to objects, event success or failure, and changes to the security or privacy posture after the event. |
Personally Identifiable Information (PII) Usage: PII should be avoided in audit records unless it is strictly necessary for operational purposes. If PII is required for a complete audit trail, a privacy risk assessment must be performed. If PII is logged, it should be masked when presented to the user through an audit log analysis tool like Splunk.
### 2. Auditable Event Requirements and Scope
Systems must be capable of auditing a minimum list of specific events, though other events that aid in the detection, tracing, and tracking of user actions and processes should also be audited.
The scope of auditing extends to events that occur in service-oriented architecture (SOA) and in distributed, transaction-based processes. This is particularly relevant to the K12 Modernization project which requires a workflow-driven approach and immutable audit logs for critical financial transitions [R.Prompt Section 1, R.Prompt Section 4].
The minimum specific events the information system must be capable of auditing include:
| Event Classification | Specific Auditable Events |
| --- | --- |
| Authorized Access | User logon/logoff; password changes; access to information, files, and systems; and remote access changes (VPN or SSH). |
| Privileged Operations | System administration activities (e.g., su or runas); account creation, modification, or deletion; and modification of access controls (permissions or privileges). |
| Unauthorized Access | All unauthorized access attempts. |
| System Alerts or Failures | System alerts, error messages, and application alerts and error messages. |
| Modification of System Security Settings | Starting and stopping of audit functions; access, modification, or deletion of audit settings; and clearing of the audit log file. |
| Modifications to Systems | Server startup and shutdown; loading and unloading of services; configuration changes; modifications to applications; installation and removal of software; changes made by a batch file; application-critical record changes; and changes to database or application records where the application layer has been bypassed (e.g., via a database utility). |
The Auditable Event Code List further details examples of events that require logging, specifically focusing on Windows Security Log events such as user account changes (Created 4720, Enabled 4722, Deleted 4726, Locked out 4740) and logon session events (Successful logon 4624, Logon failure 4625).
### 3. Audit Log Controls and Protection
Audit logs must be protected from modification, deletion, and altering the time order.
Centralized Management and Transfer: Audit logs should be transferred to a centralized log repository (like a syslog server or log aggregation tool such as Splunk) by the originating system as soon as it can reach the repository. This practice increases the confidentiality and integrity of records and ensures adequate logging space on the originating system. Splunk is specifically used by CFI for event correlation and analysis.
Failure Management:
- In case of an audit processing failure, the Security Office and key members of the IT Operations team must be alerted.

- If audit storage capacity is exceeded, systems should be configured to either overwrite the oldest event or to shut down upon failure of the audit log process.

- Centralized repositories should be configured to alert when logs from a system have not been received within a reasonable timeframe.

Security and Review:
- Privileged access to audit-related privileges must be segregated from other privileged actions.

- Logs should be analyzed and reviewed within 7 days of the auditable event for anomalies or unusual behaviors, and findings must be reported to the Security Office. The frequency of review should be increased if credible sources reveal an escalated risk.

### 4. Storage and Retention Requirements
Retention requirements define how long logs must be kept and how they must be secured.
- Audit logs must be retained for 3 years.

- Of that retention period, at least 4 months of the audit logs must be available online.

- Audit logs must be transferred off of online storage onto offline storage systems as needed.

- When stored in offline storage, audit logs must be kept on encrypted media.

- Offline audit logs must be kept at an offsite storage location.



# Internal Runbook: Azure Defender for DevOps (AzD4D)

*Created: 2025-11-10T15:14:38.965Z by Stuart Goings*
*Last Updated: 2025-11-10T21:38:41.283Z by Stuart Goings*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4445831172/Internal+Runbook+Azure+Defender+for+DevOps+AzD4D*

---

Internal documentation for enabling, operating, and continuously improving our Azure Defender for DevOps implementation across Azure DevOps projects and repos. Examples and defaults are aligned to the k12-api-enrollment repo and its multi‑stage pipeline.
## What AzD4D Covers (and what it doesn&rsquo;t)
In-scope protections
- Secret scanning (CredScan) in Pipelines and on PRs

- Binary static analysis (BinSkim) for managed/native binaries

- Open-source dependency & container scanning (Trivy)

- SARIF upload + Security tab findings in Azure DevOps

Out-of-scope (complementary controls)
- SCA licensing enforcement (use policy-as-code/gates)

- IaC misconfig (handled by separate tools: e.g., PSRule for Azure, tfsec, Checkov)

- Cloud runtime posture (handled by Defender for Cloud / Azure Policy)

## Minimum Requirements & Access
Azure:
- Azure subscription with Defender for Cloud enabled.

- Defender for DevOps plan enabled at the subscription/management group.

- RBAC: Security Admin/Reader for viewing findings; Owner/Contributor for enabling plan.

Azure DevOps (ADO):
- Organization connection to Defender for Cloud (via Defender for DevOps connector).

- Project-level permissions to manage service connections and pipelines.

- Build agents: windows-2022 or ubuntu-latest images are supported.

## Standard Pipeline Integration (Reference: k12-api-enrollment)
> Goal: Provide consistent, low‑friction security scanning on every PR and CI build, with deterministic prerequisites for CredScan.
Highlights from the reference YAML (Build stage):
- Build, SQL build, unit tests, coverage, SBOM generation

- Prereq: Install .NET 6 SDK (CredScan requirement)

- Resolve a valid DOTNET_ROOT dynamically on the hosted agent

- Run MSDO with policy azuredevops and tools credscan,binskim,trivy

- publish: true to send SARIF to ADO Security

- break: false to not fail the build (we gate in PR policies&mdash;see &sect;6)

SBOM step: Uses Microsoft SBOM Tool to generate SPDX JSON during CI. Keep -pn, -pv, -ps, -nsb aligned to our naming conventions (see &sect;8).
## Agent Prerequisites & Known Pitfalls
CredScan requires .NET 6 runtime. Ensure:
- UseDotNet@2 installs SDK 6.0.x.

- A robust PowerShell step probes typical dotnet roots and sets DOTNET_ROOT and PATH. (Included in the reference pipeline.)

Troubleshooting tips:
- If a prior step changes PATH or DOTNET_ROOT, run the dotnet info echo step again.

- For self‑hosted agents, preinstall the .NET 6 runtime and set a system‑wide DOTNET_ROOT to reduce variance.

Artifacts/paths:
- If you publish Code Analysis logs, verify PathtoPublish exists before artifact publishing. Use Write-Host (Test-Path <path>) checks to avoid spurious failures.

## Running Microsoft Security DevOps (MSDO)
Recommended defaults:
Tool selection:
- credscan: finds exposed secrets and credentials

- binskim: analyzes produced binaries for secure compiler/linker settings

- trivy: detects vulnerable OS/deps in container images and file systems

Outputs:
- SARIF uploaded to the Security tab in ADO (project level and per pipeline run)

- Optional local files in the agent&rsquo;s temp dir (for debugging). Consider publishing them as a non‑retained artifact in debug scenarios only.

## Enforcement Strategy (PR Gates vs. Build Break)
We prioritize developer experience while ensuring risk is managed through PR policies:
- Keep pipeline break: false for MSDO to avoid noisy red builds.

- Enforce PR policies per repo/branch:
Require AzD4D checks to succeed before merge

- Block PRs on High/Critical findings

- Require linked work item if suppressing a finding (see &sect;7)

> This pattern reduces friction for CI builds while maintaining security at merge boundaries.
## Findings Triage & Suppression Process
Triage queue:
- Owners: Repo Security Champions (one per team), Security Eng (backup)

- SLA targets: Critical&mdash;48h, High&mdash;5 days, Medium&mdash;30 days, Low&mdash;best effort

Workflow:
1. Classify: True positive, accepted risk, false positive

2. Fix path: Code change, rotate secret, remove binary, patch deps, or change build flags

Suppress path (rare): Use MSDO‑supported suppression where available or baseline SARIF, and always:
- Scope narrowly (file/line/rule)

- Add justification referencing a ticket and approval

- Set an expiry/review date

3. Verify: Rerun pipeline; ensure PR policy passes

CredScan secrets:
- Prefer rotating the secret and adding it to a secret store (Key Vault, variable group).

- If intentional test data, gate with deterministic patterns or test vaults; avoid real‑looking tokens.

## SBOM Policy (SPDX JSON via Microsoft SBOM Tool)
Purpose: Asset inventory for dependency risk, provenance, and incident response.
Standards:
- Format: SPDX 2.3 JSON

- Naming:
-pn: repo name ($(Build.Repository.Name)) or service name

- -pv: build/buildNumber or semver

- -ps: CloudLattice (or business unit)

- -nsb: canonical URL namespace (https://sbom.cloudlattice.io/<service>)

Storage & retention:
- Publish SBOM as part of drop artifact

- Retain minimum 90 days; mirror to long‑term artifact store if needed

Downstream use: Feed Defender for Cloud, vulnerability management, and asset owners&rsquo; dashboards.
## Notifications & Reporting
Near real‑time:
- Enable ADO notifications for Security alerts to the #sec‑alerts channel (ADO service hook → Teams/Email)

- Optional: Actionable emails to code owners on Critical/High findings

Weekly digest:
- Aggregated summary by project: new findings, closed findings, SLA breaches

Monthly review:
- Top 10 recurring rules, hotspot components, mean‑time‑to‑remediate, exception drift

## Dashboards & KQL (Starter)
Use Defender for Cloud and ADO Analytics to visualize trends.
ADO Analytics (example widgets):
- Open security findings by severity over time

- Findings by repo/service

- Age of findings (SLA burn‑down)

KQL snippets (Defender for Cloud → Workbooks):
## Operational Runbook
Onboard a new repo
1. Confirm project is linked to Defender for Cloud (AzD4D connector)

2. Add the Standard Security template to pipeline (copy MSDO + .NET6 prereqs)

3. Configure PR policies (block on High/Critical; require status checks)

4. Add SBOM step with correct naming

5. Add Security Champion and code owners file (CODEOWNERS)

Incident handling (e.g., leaked secret)
1. Containment: Disable/rotate credentials immediately (Key Vault, Azure Portal)

2. Purge: Remove secret from the repo history if committed; rotate any downstream tokens

3. Verify: Rerun pipeline; confirm finding is closed; add post‑mortem notes

Quarterly hygiene
- Review exceptions/suppressions; expire stale ones

- Update agent images and .NET LTS where applicable

- Sample audit of pipelines for missing MSDO/SBOM

## Governance & Roles
Security Engineering
- Maintain the standard pipeline template

- Own tool updates (MSDO, SBOM Tool) and global policies

- Run monthly metrics and leadership reviews

Repo Security Champion
- First responder for findings in their repos

- Coordinates fixes/suppressions and communicates in PRs

Dev Teams
- Remediate actionable findings per SLA

- Add proper tests and secure build flags

## Reference Snippets (Copy‑Paste)
.NET 6 Prereq + Robust DOTNET_ROOT Discovery
MSDO Task
SBOM (Windows)
## Quality Gates & DevEx Tips
- Keep security tasks early in the Build stage to fail fast during PRs (if PR policy chooses to block).

- Prefer comment bots (service hooks) on PRs with crisp remediation steps.

- Tag findings with service/component for faster ownership.

- Add training links for common rule families (hardcoded credentials, weak crypto, unsafe deserialization, etc.).

## Appendix
Versioning & Updates
- Pin MicrosoftSecurityDevOps@1 and revisit release notes quarterly.

- Periodically validate Trivy DB freshness (auto‑updates within the task).

Change Log
- v1.0 &ndash; Initial internal runbook aligned to k12-api-enrollment pipeline.



# K12Technology Roadmap

*Created: 2025-11-11T14:50:32.662Z by Matt Vaughn*
*Last Updated: 2025-11-11T14:51:31.919Z by Matt Vaughn*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4450877441/K12Technology+Roadmap*

---

## K12 Modernization
IT Stakeholder Presentation
October 2025- College Foundation, Inc.

- North Carolina SEAA Programs

- Timeline: October 15, 2025 - May 1, 2026

## Agenda
1. Executive Summary &ndash; Project overview and timeline
Modernizaton Scope

2. Core Capabilities

3. Platform Overview &ndash; Complete system scope

4. Tools

5. Infrastructure

6. Cross-Cutting Concerns

7. 3rd-Party Integrations

8. Data Migrations

Complete Feature Inventory &ndash; All core capabilities
Risk Assessment &ndash; Critical areas and mitigation
Success Criteria &ndash; Delivery milestones and quality metrics
Next Steps &ndash; Execution and monitoring
## Executive Summary
### Complete K12 Modernization Scope
Timeline: October 15, 2025 - May 1, 2026 (28 weeks)
Platform Serves:
- Two scholarship programs (ESA+, Opportunity Scholarship)

- Up to 80,000 concurrent users

- Four web portals (Household, School, Provider, Admin)

- Comprehensive scholarship lifecycle management

### Core Capabilities Required
1. Household & Student Management (15+ features): This domain covers the core entry points for end-users. Critical features include the ESA+ and OS application workflows, renewal processes, and student profile and account management.

2. School Management (20+ features): This focuses on institutional requirements, including school registration and profiles, annual certification workflows, and the crucial, time-sensitive semester parent endorsement process.

3. Provider Management (12+ features): This involves managing service providers (tutors, therapists) who accept ESA+ funds. Key features include provider registration and enrollment, the public provider directory, and invoice submission and processing.

4. Admin Portal Operations (30+ features): This is the operational core for SEAA staff, enabling application processing and review, verification management (domicile, income, sampling), award management (lottery, calculation, lifecycle), and necessary reporting and analytics.

5. Payment & Financial Systems (18+ features): This domain handles the disbursement of scholarship funds. Critical requirements include direct payment school processing, ESA+ wallet and purchasing (which relies heavily on ClassWallet integration), tax reporting (1099-G), rollover management, and minimum spending enforcement ($1,000 threshold for ESA+).

6. Verification & Compliance (15+ features): This domain is critical for regulatory adherence and includes eligibility verification workflows, ongoing compliance monitoring, and integrations with 7 state agencies. The mandatory process of verification sampling (4% of applications) and handling non-cooperation falls here.

Critical Deadline: May 1, 2026 - All core functionality must be production-ready

## K-12 SEAA Modernization Project: Technology Roadmap Report
### 1. Definition of a Technology Roadmap
A Technology Roadmap, in the context of the K-12 SEAA Modernization project, is a strategic, phased delivery plan covering the entire scope of work, designed to guide the development teams toward a critical production launch deadline of May 1, 2026.
Based on the materials, the roadmap functions as the implementation plan, detailing the sequential delivery of 110+ distinct features across six core functional domains, the integration of high-risk external systems (like ClassWallet), and the resolution of critical architectural and data integrity deficiencies. The roadmap prioritizes the delivery of cross-cutting services early to ensure the foundation for security (Entra ID), logic (Rules Engine), and communication (Communication Center) is stable before executing advanced administrative and financial workflows.
### 2. Project Context and State of Project
The K-12 SEAA Modernization project has been in development for approximately one year. The project serves two scholarship programs (ESA+ and Opportunity Scholarship) and must support up to 80,000 concurrent users across four web portals (Household, School, Provider, Admin).
#### Core Features
- Status: NOT IMPLEMENTED (NI)

- Challenges: None of the core features are implemented, integrated and/or complete.
Registration and application workflows for ESA+ and Opportunity Scholarship

- Renewal processes

- School and Provider management portals

- Admin portal operations for SEAA staff

- Payment processing and financial systems

- Verification and compliance workflows, including state agency integrations

- Database schema for all domains (modernization)

Resolution:
- Implement workflow-centric operations to deliver core features in a phased manner over the next 7 months.

- Start with foundational features (i.e., Student, Household, School, Provider) and logical sequence(s)
Stop focus on tool-building

- Stop focus on "what is easy" (e.g., Provider)

Focus on event-driven workflows to decouple services and improve scalability
- REQUIRES: grouping tasks to operational workflows with application context

Prioritize high-risk external integrations (ClassWallet, RDS) early in the roadmap
Need application database and resources to enforce relational integrity and domain-driven design
- Implement a coordination of database schemas, changes, and environment deployments

#### Architecture (Backend)
- Status: NI / IN PROGRESS (IP)

- Challenges:
Process: Architecture Design, Analysis, and Decision Records (NI)

- Business Logic Layer (NI)

- Workflow and Event-driven Processing (IP)

- Strong Business Logic with Rules Engine (IP)

Resolution:
- Complete Architecture Decision Records (ADRs) for all major design decisions

- Finalize Business Logic Layer design using Rules Engine

- Fully implement event-driven architecture with Workflow-centric operations and Task Engine
REQUIRES: grouping tasks to operational workflows with application context

#### Architecture (Frontend)
- Status: New

- Challenges:
(4) autonomous teams working in silos without architectural governance

- Consistent components communication (i.e., API, responses ) patterns not established

- Enterprise cross-cutting concerns (i.e., logging, error handling, telemetry) not implemented/standardized

Resolution:
- (1) Frontend Architect to coordinate implementation of patterns, components, and shared libraries
Adding core cross-cutting concerns (i.e., logging, error handling, and configuration)

Document and socialize standards for API contracts, responses, and error handling
Establish architectural governance process for all teams to follow
Implement shared libraries for cross-cutting concerns and utilities
- shared models and DTO library projects

- shared layout and styles/assets library projects

- Data access layer with repository pattern (i.e., Http Service)

#### Cross-Cutting Services
- Status: DONE / IP

- Details:
Microsoft Entra (Security) (DONE)

- Communication Center (DONE)

- SignalR (DONE)

- PandaDocs (DONE)

- Document Storage (DONE)

- Rules Engine (IP)

- Observability/Telemetry (IP).

Resolution:
- Complete implementation and integration of Rules Engine with business logic

- Finalize Observability/Telemetry for end-to-end monitoring and diagnostics
AppInsights, Log Analytics, Distributed Tracing

- by Application (i.e., frontend and backend), environment

- monitoring dashboards and alerts (thresholds)

#### 3rd-Party Integrations
- Status: NI / DONE / IP

- Details:
SparkPost (Email Engine) (DONE)

- PandaDocs (DONE)

- ClassWallet (NI)

- State Agency Integrations (RDS) (NI)

- Aperio/CISCO Call Center (NI)

Resolution:

- Establish integration patterns and error handling strategies for external systems

#### Data & Data Migration

Migrations for Schools (NI)

- Providers (NI)

- Household

- Students (NI)

- and Operational Data (NI) are all pending

Resolution:
- Implement and/or stabilize the modernization schema(s)
Need resource(s) to lead schema design and enforcement

Develop comprehensive data migration strategy and tools
- Need resource(s) to lead schema design and enforcement

- Prioritize migrations based on feature delivery roadmap to ensure data availability for core functionalities

|
### 3. Critical Risk Assessment and Mitigation
The project faces several HIGH RISK areas that could jeopardize the May 1, 2026 deadline.
| Critical Risk | Challenge | Mitigation Strategy |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ClassWallet Integration | High vendor dependency for all ESA+ wallet, purchasing, and expense management workflows in Phase 4. | Use Idempotency keys for all transactions and perform rigorous sandbox testing early. |
| Database Schemas | High risk due to lack of resources and/or expertise in modernizing legacy schemas. | Execute a Rapid Assessment and Remediation plan to identify and address schema issues in parallel with development efforts. Focus on foundational data schemas first. |
| Legacy Data Migration | High risk due to legacy data quality, volume, and the current schema's lack of relational integrity. | Execute a Phased Migration by Bounded Context rather than a single "Big Bang" approach to reduce risk and allow for iterative validation. |



# Household & Student Management (15+ features)

*Created: 2025-11-11T20:49:06.898Z by Matt Vaughn*
*Last Updated: 2025-11-11T20:49:55.893Z by Matt Vaughn*
*URL: https://cfi-nc.atlassian.net/wiki/spaces/KR/pages/4450517067/Household+Student+Management+15+features*

---

> The Household & Student Management (HSM) domain is the foundational entry point for all end-users of the K12/SEAA system and comprises more than 15 features.
## Overview
The Household & Student Management domain includes workflows that guide parents and students through the entire scholarship lifecycle, beginning with the initial application and continuing through fund spending, compliance monitoring, and annual renewal. This domain covers the core entry points for end-users, including ESA+ and Opportunity Scholarship (OS) application workflows, renewal processes, and student profile and account management.
This domain is a key focus of Phase 1: Foundation & Core Applications (Weeks 1-8), dedicated to delivering the core application forms using Angular reactive forms to meet the May 1, 2026 hard deadline.
## Categorized Breakdown of Workflows
The Household & Student Management domain organizes critical user interactions into three main workflow categories:
### Application and Onboarding Workflows
- ESA+ New Application Submission: The workflow for submitting an ESA+ application, which includes inputting student eligibility information, uploading the Eligibility Determination Document (EDD), verifying domicile, and confirming prior public school enrollment.

- Opportunity Scholarship New Application Submission: The process that captures student demographics, household composition, income data for tier calculation, and domicile information, leading to lottery participation.

- Application Status Tracking and Notifications: The feature that allows the household user to monitor the current state of their application (e.g., draft, submitted, eligible, waitlisted).

- Award Offer Acceptance/Decline: The workflow through which parents acknowledge receipt of an award offer and formally accept or decline the scholarship by the specified deadline.

- Parent Agreement and LEA Release Signature: The crucial process requiring parents to electronically sign the annual Parent Agreement and the Local Education Agency (LEA) Release (if attending a full-time nonpublic or home school) before funds are activated.

##### Lifecycle, Renewal, and Compliance Workflows
- Annual Renewal Application Submission: The simplified workflow used by existing recipients of both OS and ESA+ to re-qualify for the subsequent program year, which includes confirming household and enrollment status.

- ESA+ Continuing Eligibility Re-evaluation: The specific task flow required every three years to submit fresh documentation of a student's disability (IEP/Eligibility Determination) to maintain eligibility.

- Minimum Spending Verification Check: The end-of-year process that checks if the ESA+ student has met the required annual spending threshold of $1,000 on allowable expenses; failure results in non-renewal.

- Parent/Household Profile Management: Allowing users to update contact information, household member details, communication preferences, and account security settings (like MFA).

- School Selection and Transfers: The feature enabling parents to search for eligible schools, select a school option (Direct Payment, Reimbursement, Home School), and submit mid-year transfer requests.

- Parent Endorsement (Semesterly): The workflow where the parent is required to electronically confirm the student&rsquo;s enrollment and authorize the disbursement of funds to a Direct Payment School each semester (Fall and Spring).

##### ESA+ Purchasing and Fund Management Workflows
